Week	1:	Common	cold	&	Influenza	• Common	cold	vs.	influenza	s Attack	by	pathogenic	wind	in	East	Asian	medicine,	and	virus	in	Western	medicine	s Clinical	signs	and	sx	include	nasal	discharge,	sneezing,	headache,	chills,	fever,	fatigue,	body	aches.	s More	severe	signs	and	sx	associated	w/	influenza	(caused	by	a	different	virus	than	the	common	cold)	• Cold	Viruses	s Rhinoviruses,	coronaviruses,	and	the	respiratory	syncytial	virus	(RSV)	s Rhinovirus	group	causes	10%	to	40%	of	colds	§ Most	active	in	early	fall,	spring,	and	summer	§ More	than	110	distinct	rhinovirus	types	have	been	identified	§ Grow	best	at	temperatures	of	about	91	degrees		s Coronaviruses	and	rsv	responsible	for	20%	and	10%	of	cases,	respectively.		s Coronaviruses	cause	many	adult	colds	§ Most	active	in	the	winter	and	early	spring	§ Three	or	four	of	the	30+	types	infect	humans	§ SARS	is	due	to	a	coronavirus	• Influenza	viruses	s Three	types,	A,	B,	and	C	s A	and	B	most	severe,	and	change	continually,	circulate	around	the	globe	s C	mild	or	asymptomatic,	does	not	cause	epidemics	and	does	not	present	a	public	health	threat	• Drift	and	shift	s Antigenic	drift	§ Small	changes	that	happen	continually	§ Produces	new	strains		§ Yearly	updates	in	the	influenza	vaccine	address	changes	d/t	drift	§ Affects	both	A	and	B	types	s Antigenic	shift	§ Sudden	major	change	in	the	influenza	A	viruses	§ Usually	no	human	immunity		§ Happens	only	occasionally	• Comparison	of	cold	and	flu	sx	
	• Complications	s Common	cold	§ Better	w/i	days	to	weeks	§ Sinus,	ear	infections;	bronchitis	s Influenza	§ Worsening	of	chronic	medical	conditions	such	as	asthma,	congestive	heart	failure,	diabetes	§ Sinus,	ear	infections;	bronchitis	§ Pneumonia;	dehydration;	death	• Peak	Month	of	Flu	Activity:		February	• Humidity	and	cold	viruses	s Cold-causing	viruses	survive	better	when	humidity	is	low.	s Cold	weather	also	can	dry	the	mucus	membranes	of	the	nose,	making	it	more	vulnerable	to	infection.	
• Mini	case	study:	cold	or	flu?	s A	mother	calls	you	up	asking	for	advice.		Her	11	year	old	daughter	has	had	a	104°	F	for	three	days,	brought	down	to	102°	w/	acetaminophen.		Her	Sx	include	severe	fatigue,	aches	and	chills.		She	doesn’t	want	to	take	her	child	to	the	doctor	for	just	a	cold.			§ What	do	you	think?		Is	it	probably	cold	or	flu?	Flu	§ How	would	you	advise	this	person?	Go	to	the	doctor	[Dehydration:		Fever	for	more	than	3	days]	• Mini	case	study:	flu	vaccination	s For	several	months	you’ve	been	treating	a	four	year	old	boy	with	respiratory	problems.		He	has	a	Dx	of	bronchomalacia,	and	an	Hx	of	frequent	pneumonia.		Flu	season	is	coming	up,	and	his	parents	want	to	know	if	they	should	have	him	get	a	flu	vaccine.	§ What	do	you	think?	No	§ What	do	you	say?	Yes,	because	liability	§ What	might	you	say	if	you	were	an	ND	or	MD/L.Ac.?	Yes	• TCM	etiology	and	pathogenesis	s Two	broad	categories	§ Pathogenic	wind	• Air	travel	• Closed	ventilation	office	buildings	• Hotel	pillows	and	blankets	• Playgrounds,	classrooms	§ Deficiency	syndromes	• Children	six	months	to	five	years	old	• Adults	50	years	or	older	• Chronic	illness	• Pathogenic	wind	(exterior	conditions)	s Wind	cold,	Wind	heat,	Wind	dampness	s

	 Week	1:	Common	cold	&	Influenza	• TCM	nutritional	therapy	(Most	ancient	tool)	s TCM	nutritional	therapy	uses	the	same	Ddx	and	Tx	principles	as	acupuncture	and	herbal	therapy.	s By	applying	the	actions	of	the	flavors	of	foods,	and	their	temperatures,	food	is	used	as	medicine.			• Flavors	and	organ	systems	s “Sourness	enters	the	Liver,		§ [Sour	à	Pucker	à	Contraction	à	LV	Qi	Stagnation]	§ Little	bit	of	lemon	juice	in	water	is	good	to	kickstart	digestion	s Bitterness	enters	the	Heart,	§ Dark	greens	à	Build	blood	§ Coffee	à	Little	bit	lifts	Heart	Qi,	but	too	much	exhausts	Heart	Qi	s Sweetness	enters	the	Spleen	§ Fruit		§ Rice,	chicken	(sweetest	of	meats),	squash	§ Too	much	sweet	goes	to	middle	&	sticks	there	à	Creates	Damp	s Acridity	enters	the	Lungs		§ [Pungent:		wasabi]	à	Goes	up	and	out	s Saltiness	enters	the	Kidneys.	§ Leaches	&	drains,	sucks	water	out	&	drains	away	à	dries	§ Too	much	salt	dries	it	out	à	Can	drain	KD	Qi	§ A	little	bit	of	salt	(seafood)	tonifies	KD	§ Lamb:		Yang	food	s Sourness	enters	the	sinews,	s Bitterness	enters	the	Blood,	[Heart	is	emperor	of	Blood]	s Acridity	enters	Qi,	s Sweetness	enters	the	flesh,	s Saltiness	enters	the	bones.”			Huang	Di	Nei	Jing	• The	five	temperatures	s Hot,	Warm,	Neutral,	Cool,	Cold	s Most	food	should	be	cool,	neutral,	warm	s Yin	Xu:		Recommend	Cool	s Antibiotics:		Very	Cold	à	Warm	food:		soup,	stew,	ginger,	probiotics,	alium	• Mini	case	studies:	nutritional	therapy	s What	flavors	and	temperatures	might	you	recommend	for	someone	with	chronic	lung	qi	deficiency?	§ Which	foods	could	satisfy	the	resulting	treatment	principles?	∗ Pungent,	acrid,	naturally	sweet	(fire	cider)	s What	flavors	and	temperatures	would	be	suitable	for	someone	with	a	wind	cold?	s What	are	some	examples	of	good	foods	in	such	a	case?	§ Fire	cider,	chicken	soup	[warm,	salty,	sweet	(chicken),	pungent]	§ Induce	sweat:		Ginger,	garlic,	mustard	bath	§ Soaking	feet	in	warm	salty	water:		Tonifies	Kidney

	 Insomnia	Definition	• Insomnia	is	being	unable	to	get	enough	good	sleep.		• Manifestations	of	insomnia	include	an	inability	to	fall	asleep,	remain	asleep,	or	feel	refreshed	by	sleep.		• Causes	constant	fatigue,	anxiety,	and	possibly	psychiatric	and	other	disorders,	such	as	delayed	tissue	repair.		Causes	• If	caused	by	temporary	emotional	factors	such	as	excitement,	terror,	grief,	or	by	environmental	factors,	such	as	noise,	bright	light,	and	there	are	no	other	symptoms,	it	will	be	a	self-limiting	condition.		• If	caused	by	other	illnesses	such	as	fever,	cough,	pain,	these	should	be	treated	first.

• Du	20,	Ren	12,	UB	20,	ST	36,		• ST	40	[best	for	expelling	damp]	• GB	20	&	GB	21	[Drain	channel]	Shen-nong.com	Analysis:	in	traditional	Chinese	medicine	(TCM)	theory,	the	bladder	meridian	governs	the	body	surface.	When	external	wind	and	cold	invade	the	body,	the	meridian	is	usually	affected,	resulting	in	discomforts	along	its	pathway	such	as	pain	in	the	head	and	neck.	The	wind	and	cold	pathogens	hinder	the	protective	qi	flowing	on	the	body	surface,	so	there	are	aversion	to	wind,	chills	and	general	aches.	The	tongue	coating	and	the	pulse	also	indicate	that	the	pathogens	are	located	in	the	exterior	portion	of	the	body.	Placing	more	than	one	needle	in	a	channel,	it	is	better	for	draining	that	entire	channel	[Helps	when	they’re	within	some	proximity]	Order	of	needling	along	the	flow	of	Qi		Electro	should	be	within	3	cun	for	best	effects

Nutritional	factor	in	the	etiology	of	the	premenstrual	tension	syndromes.	J	Reprod	Med	1983;	28:	446-464)	• 	PMS-A	(A	=	anxiety)	s Most	common	symptom	category	s Strongly	associated	with	excessive	estrogen	and	deficient	progesterone	during	premenstrual	phase	s Common	symptoms	are	anxiety,	irritability,	and	emotional	ability	
• PMS-C	(C	=	carbohydrate	craving)	s Associated	with	increased	appetite,	craving	for	sweets,	headache,	fatigue,	fainting,	and	heart	palpitations	• Glucose	tolerance	tests	during	the	5-10	days	before	menses	suggest	excessive	secretion	of	insulin	in	response	to	sugar	consumption;	during	other	parts	of	the	menstrual	cycle	GTT	is	normal. Causes	redness,	swelling	&	itching	Differentiation	• Abrupt	onset	w/	itchy,	raised,	red/light	red	wheals	that	may	vary	in	size	&	shape	or	with	pimples	rising	one	after	another	• Hives	may	appear	on	any	part	of	body	with	one	or	many	areas	• Most	common	on	arms,	legs,	trunk	[Could	be	anywhere]	• May	be	aggravated	or	alleviated	by	weather	change	• Rash	may	last	for	a	few	minutes	or	several	days	• Acute	conditions	subside	quickly	• Severe	reaction:		bee	sting	à	face	&	throat	may	swell	à	Call	911	• Rarely,	hives	may	cause	problems	with	breathing,	creating	danger	of	a	severe	asthma	attack	or	a	closing	of	throat	from	swelling	• Divided	into	following	types	according	to	clinical	manifestations:	Types	 Clinical	Manifestations	 Supplementary	Points	Wind	Heat	(Wind	à	quick)		 Red	Rashes	with	severe	itching. Wind	Damp	White	or	light	red	rashes	accompanied	by	heaviness	of	body	[Looks	wet,	water	inside]	T:		White	&	sticky	tongue	coat	P:		Floating	&	slow	pulse	
SP	9	[Transform	Damp]	
Heat	accumulates	in	ST	&	Intestines		• Food	allergies	• Parasites	• 	
Red	rashes	accompanied	by	abdominal	or	epigastric	pain,	constipation	or	diarrhea	• Diarrhea:		Intestines	inflamed	due	to	allergies,	but	still	have	room	for	the	water	• Constipation:		Intestines	swollen	inward	à	close	up,	nothing	can	pass	through	T:		Thin,	yellow	coat	P:		Rapid	
Promote	bowel	movements:	• ST	25,	ST	37	• SJ	6,	UB	25	
Diagnosis	Find	out	history	of	sensitivity	to	such	things	as:	• Food	[Especially	shellfish,	eggs,	milk,	nuts,	berries,	dyes	or	other	additives]	• Plants	[nettles],	pollens	• Medicines	[Penicillin,	aspirin,	sulfa]	• Animal	fur,	saliva	• Insect	bites,	stings	• Exposure	to	heat,	cold,	sunshine		Additional	Considerations	• Easiest	to	identify	drugs,	foods	or	plants	that	may	cause	hives	because	the	response	usually	occurs	within	an	hour.	• Triggers	such	as	emotional	stress	or	multiple	allergies	may	take	more	time	&	require	skin	tests	or	other	types	of	tests.	• Suggest	that	patient	keep	a	detailed	diary	of	anything	they	eat,	drink	or	are	exposed	to	for	2-4	weeks	• Avoid	suspect	foods,	then	add	back	in	one	at	a	time	• See	allergist	for	skin	tests	
Additional	Treatment	• Avoid	heat	or	rubbing,	which	releases	more	histamines	• Take	antihistamine	medicine	to	reduce	allergic	response	• For	severe	cases,	take	oral	steroids	(e.g.	prednisone)	• For	emergency	cases	that	cause	hives	to	spread	quickly,	may	require	a	shot	of	antihistamine	such	as	Benadryl	• If	any	suspicion	that	throat	is	swelling	or	patient	is	having	trouble	breathing	or	wheezing,	administer	epinephrine	(adrenaline)	to	counteract	reaction.	• For	life	threatening	cases,	call	911	or	refer	to	ER.

	Herpes	Zoster	[Shingles]	Definition	• Herpes	Zoster	is	virus	&	Urticaria	is	allergy	• Word	shingles	comes	from	Latin	word	for	belt	or	girdle	• Infection	caused	by	virus	[varicella	zoster]	that	causes	chickenpox	s More	painful	than	itchy	s Often	located	along	nerves	s Usually	half	circle	s Heat	&	fire	s Occurs	mainly	in	lumbar	&	hypochondriac	region	with	small	red	vesicles	like	beads	forming	a	girdle	around	the	waist	• TCM:		“Snake	Herpes”	s Caused	by	endogenous	damp	heat,	LV	&	GB	Fire	or	exogenous	toxin	s Mostly	occurs	in	spring	&	fall	s Most	common	in	people	over	60	year	old,	but	young	people	can	have	it	also	s Cannot	develop	shingles	unless	have	previous	infection	of	chickenpox			Etiology	&	Pathology	• TCM	Causes	s Damp	Heat	Accumulation	[Toxin]:	Virus	hiding	inside,	triggered	by	Qi	Xu	s Qi	Xu	s LV	&	GB	Fire	[Emotional:		Sadness,	grief]	§ LV	insults	SP	&	ST	à	Affects	formation	of	Qi	&	Blood	à	Disturbs	eating	&	sleeping	à	Don’t	worry,	Be	Happy!	• Cause	s Chicken	Pox	virus	is	not	destroyed	after	patients	recover	from	chicken	pox	s Virus	moves	back	to	roots	of	nerve	cells	(near	spinal	cord)	&	becomes	inactive	(dormant)	s Later,	if	virus	is	reactivated,	symptoms	are	called	shingles	• Western	Medicine:		Exact	cause	of	virus	reactivation	is	not	known	• Commonly	Observed	Triggers	s Weakened	immune	system	[Immune	suppression	medicines	(organ	transplant),	Illness,	major	surgery,	complication	of	cancer	or	AIDS]	s Advancing	age	&	chronic	use	of	cortisone-type	drugs	§ Long	term	steroid	use	can	cause	decreased	immunity,	moon	face,	fat	accumulation	on	back	of	neck,	osteoporosis	§ Western	doctors	like	to	use	it	because	it	masks	symptoms	§ Steroids	have	many	side	effects	s Injury	to	skin	or	sunburn	s Emotional	Stress	
Differentiation	• Onset:		Burning,	sharp,	or	stabbing	pain,	tingling	or	numbness	in	or	under	skin	on	one	side	of	body	or	face	• Most	common	site	is	back	or	upper	abdomen	• Typical	distribution	looks	like	a	belt,	which	becomes	red	• May	have	slight	fever,	chills,	headache,	lassitude,	poor	appetite,	nausea	• Rash	of	fluid-filled	blisters	on	reddened	skin	in	size	of	grain	or	soybeans	à	form	band-like	distribution	(skin	eruptions	occur	in	separate	patches,	forming	a	girdle)	with	clear	cut	interspaces	between	the	patches	• Blisters	are	thick-walled	and	contents	are	clear	or	transparent	at	first,	then	turn	turbid	within	3-4	days.		Blisters	turn	yellow,	then	dry	&	crust	over	in	5-6	days	• Resolution	of	cutaneous	lesions	after	decrustation	(crusts	drop	off)	without	scar	formation	in	about	2	weeks	[Often	takes	much	longer	to	heal]	• Sometimes	small,	pitted	scars	remain	[If	rash	is	broken]	• In	some	cases,	pain,	numbness	or	tingling	lasts	longer	[Months	à	years]	if	virus	causes	nerve	damage	[Post-herpetic	neuralgia]	s Most	likely	to	occur	in	patients	>	50	years	old	• Blisters	tend	to	follow	nerve	paths	in	a	line	à	Usually	found	just	on	one	side	of	body	along	intercostal	nerves	[extending	from	back	or	flank	around	to	the	abdomen]	à	Usually	don’t	cross	midline	of	body	• Rash	may	appear	on	one	side	of	face	• Some	patients	may	have	eye	inflammations	&	infections	• Patients	with	shingles	should	avoid	contact	with	infants,	children,	pregnant	women	&	adults	who	have	never	had	chickenpox	or	chickenpox	vaccine	until	blisters	are	completely	dry	because	it	is	infectious	&	can	spread	by	direct	contact	with	a	blister.		Diagnosis	• Ask	about	symptoms	&	Examine	diseased	area	• Ask	if	patient	has	hx	of	chickenpox	• Lab	tests	to	look	for	virus	in	fluid	from	a	blister	may	be	necessary		Treatment	[WM]	• Painkillers	[acetaminophen,	GABA]	• Cool,	moist	washcloths	on	rash	• Antibacterial	salves,	lotions	to	prevent	bacterial	infection	of	blisters	• Capsaicin	cream	for	pain	• Corticosteroids	[if	>	50	years	old]	Types	[2]	Symptoms	 Treatment	Principles	Points	Spleen	Damp	Heat	• Yellow	or	white	soybean	sized	blisters,	erosion,	pronounced	pain	• Spleen	Damp	Heat	Symptoms	 Clear	Heat	&	Transform	damp	• SP	9,	SP	6:		Spleen	T&T	functions	• GB	34,	SJ	6:		Heat,	promote	bowel	movements	[Most	efficient	way	to	get	rid	of	heat]	• Ashi	LV	&	GB	Fire	• Flushed	skin,	densely	distributed	skin	eruptions	in	patches,	burning	heat,	stabbing	pain,	absence	of	erosion	• LV	&	GB	Symptoms	 Clear	Fire	in	LV	&	GB	• LV	3,	LV2,	GB	34,	SJ	6	LI	11,	Ashi	
Secondary	points	• Blood	points		s SP	10	[Cool]	s UB	40	[Blood	Xi	Cleft	à	Deeper	level	to	move	&	cool	blood]:			• LI	4,	SJ	5	[3	hand	yang]:		Move	à			• Points	around	rash		Special	Technique:		Cut	off	both	snake	head	&	tail	• Snake	Tail:		Area	where	skin	lesions	first	appeared	• Snake	Head:		Extending	part	of	herpes	• Procedure:		Prick	skin	around	herpes	zoster	with	3	edged	needle	[Reducing	technique:		larger	gauge]	to	cause	a	little	bleeding	[clear	out	heat	&	toxins]	s 5	pricks	at	0.5	cun	from	head	area	s 5	pricks	at	0.5	cun	from	tail	area	s Several	pricks	along	both	sides

		Psoriasis	[Yin	Xue	Bing]	Definition	• TCM:		Cow	Skin	Tinea	[Niu	Pi	Xian]	à	Like	cow	skin	(thick	&	tough)	• Doesn’t	respond	to	any	treatment	AT	ALL!!!	Many	factors	involved	s Immunity,	emotions,	genetics,	environment	• Very	hard	to	treat	&	cure	• In	Yang	(dark)	places	• More	toxic,	more	genetic	• Damage	skin	in	layers:		Top	à	White,	Underneath	à	Red,	purple,	brown	s Top:		Instead	of	keratin,	has	scales	s Underneath:		Plaques	s Base:		Red,	inflamed	skin	• More	peel	à	Get	worse	è	Don’t	scratch	or	peel!!!	• Characteristics:	s Common	chronic,	recurrent	disease	characterized	by	dry,	well-circumscribed	(sharply	defined	red	patches	covered	by	a	silvery	surface),	silvery,	scaling	papules	&	plaques	of	various	sizes	s Varies	in	severity	from	1-2	lesions	to	widespread	dermatosis,	sometimes	associated	with	disabling	arthritis	or	exfoliation.		s About	2-4%	of	whites	and	far	fewer	blacks	are	affected.	s Onset	usually	between	ages	10-40,	but	no	age	exempt.	s Family	history	is	common.	• Psoriatic	Arthritis	[usually	young	20-40]:		attacks	soft	tissues		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Channels:		LV,	LU,	SP,	ST	• Wind	Damp	Heat	in	skin	blocks	the	channels	&	collaterals	• Locations:		Scalp,	elbow,	knee	&	sacrum	[Yang	meridians	à	too	many	toxins]	• WM:		Cause	unknown	à	Treatment:		Steroids,	sleeping	pills	s Response	of	psoriasis	to	immunosuppressive	drug	cyclosporine	suggests	that	the	primary	pathogenic	factor	may	be	immunologic	[possibly	autoimmune	like	lupus,	asthma,	Sjogren	(dry	eye	syndrome)	à	Venus	Williams	(tennis)]	§ Overactive	immune	system	attacking	self	tissues	s Thick	scaling	has	traditionally	been	attributed	to	increased	epidermal	cell	proliferation	&	concomitant	dermal	inflammation	s Precipitating	factors	for	flares:		local	trauma,	occasionally	irritation,	severe	sunburn,	viremia,	allergic	drug	reactions,	topical	&	systemic	drugs	(chloroquine	anti-malarial	therapy,	lithium,	B-Blockers,	interferon),	withdrawal	of	systemic	corticosteroids.		Manifestations	• Usually	gradual	onset,	exacerbated	by	STRESS!!!	• Typical	course:		chronic	remissions	&	recurrences	or	occasionally	acute	exacerbations	that	vary	in	frequency	&	duration	• Characteristically	involves	scalp	(including	post-auricular	regions),	extensor	surface	of	extremities	(especially	elbows	&	knees),	sacral	area,	buttocks,	penis.	• Behind	ear	very	common,	knee,	elbow,	nape	of	neck	• May	also	affect	nails,	eyebrows,	axillae,	umbilicus	or	anogenital	region	• Occasionally	generalized	• Typical	lesions	are	sharply	demarcated,	variously	pruritic,	ovoid	or	circinate	erythematous	papules	with	plaques	covered	with	overlapping	thick,	silvery	micaceous	or	slightly	opalescent	shiny	scales.		Papules	sometimes	extend	&	coalesce	to	produce	large	plaques	in	annular	&	gyrate	patterns	• Very	Important:		Don’t	scrape	off	white	layer!!!	Red	layer	will	show	up	and	stimulate	immune	system	
Characteristics	• Emotions	make	it	worse,	Aggravated	or	recurrence	with	emotional	stimulation	• Affected	skin	itches,	worse	at	night	• Rash	with	scratching,	affected	skin	gets	brown	• Repeated	recurrences	make	skin	of	psoriatic	patches	thick,	dry	&	tough,	lichenized,	often	symmetric,	skin	crumbs	fall	off		Secondary	Points	• Ashi	Points	[where	problem	located]	• Severe	itching	&	irritability:		KD	6	(Emotional	point),	HT	7	[Itching,	irritability]		s [KD	7	&	HT	6	à	Night	Sweating]	• Nape:		LU	7,	UB	40	• Elbow:		LU	5,	PC	4,	PC	8	[Along	meridian]	• Popliteal	Fossa:		UB	37,	UB	40,	UB	60	• Medial	aspect	of	thigh:		SP	10		• Upper	eye	lid:		ST	8,	DU	20		s Eye	no	longer	symmetrical	• Additional:		4	needles	at	4	corners	surrounding	area	of	psoriasis	s Not	directly	on	skin	lesions	s Needles	criss	cross	underneath	transversely	to	get	rid	of	toxins	from	the	bottom		Other	Therapies	• Moxa:		once	twice	daily	[Use	real	moxa	à	Aromatic,	smoky	function]	s Qing	Hao,	Yin	Chen	Hao	à	Clear	damp	heat	s Smokeless	is	already	burned	à	Doesn’t	work	as	well	• Seven	Star	needles	&	cupping,	once	every	other	day	à	Problem	on	meridians	s Elbow,	etc.	s Not	directly	on	skin	lesions	• Ear	acupuncture,	Lung,	Shen	Men,	Adrenal,	Subcortex,	Liver,	Ashi	• Topical	Herbs	s Acute:		Di	Yu	[Stop	Bleeding,	Inflammation,	clear	heat],	Huang	Bai	s Di	Yu,	Duan	Shi	Gao	[Astringent],	Ming	Fan	(Alum)	• Comprehensive	CHM	Treatment:		CHM	pages	381-382		Diagnosis	• May	be	confused	with	seborrheic	dermatitis	but	diagnosis	by	inspection	is	rarely	difficult:	s Psoriasis:		well-defined,	dry,	heaped-up,	psoriatic	lesions,	large	silvery	scales	s Seborrheic	dermatitis:		Diffuse,	greasy,	yellowish	scaling	• Biopsy	findings	of	typical	lesions	are	generally	characteristic	• Atypical	lesions	have	atypical	features	à	biopsy	less	helpful	• Some	other	skin	disorders	may	have	psoriasisiform	histologic	features	that	may	make	microscopic	diagnosis	difficult	or	equivocal		Prognosis	• Depends	on	extent	&	severity	of	initial	involvement.		Usually	the	earlier	the	age	of	onset,	the	greater	the	severity	• Acute	attacks	usually	clear,	but	permanent	remission	is	rare		Treatment	[WM]	• No	therapy	is	curative,	but	most	cases	adequately	or	well	controlled	• Lubricants,	keratolytics,	topical	corticosteroids,	topical	vitamin	D	derivatives,	anthralin	(Dritho-Scalp,	Drithocreme)	should	be	tried	in	patients	with	a	limited	number	of	lesions	• Exposure	to	sunlight	beneficial,	but	sunburn	may	exacerbate	• Systemic	anti-metabolites	should	only	be	used	in	patients	with	severe	skin	or	joint	involvement. 		Acne	Definition	• Known	as	adolescent	rash	[Chinese:	adolescent	dou]	• Usually	occurs	on	face,	back	&	chest	• Skin	condition	that	occurs	when	the	oil-secreting	glands	in	the	skin	&	at	the	base	of	hairs	are	clogged	&	become	inflamed	or	infected	• 90%	of	teenagers	get	acne	• Usually	occurs	on	face,	upper	part	of	chest	&	back	or	shoulders	• At	first,	only	small	rashes	appear	in	the	pores	of	the	skin,	some	with	blackheads	(open,	plugged	oil	glands),	some	with	sebaceous	powder	• If	accompanied	by	infection,	purulent	sores,	knots	or	sebaceous	cysts	could	appear.		Brown	scars	could	be	left	after	rashes	gone.	• Two	Types	[Both	Yin	Deficiency]	s Adolescent	or	climacteric	acne:		Mainly	caused	by	disorder	of	Chong	&	Ren	channels.	§ Teenagers:		Growing	so	much	à	Too	much	Yang	(Yin	insufficient,	lags	behind	the	Yang),	not	enough	sleep,	poor	food	choices	à	blockages	à	folliculitis,	hormones	release	a	lot	of	oil	onto	skin	s Oil	Acne:		Mainly	caused	by	Damp	Heat	of	SP	&	ST	§ Related	to	lifestyle	(heat,	damp	food)		Etiology	• During	teenage	years,	hormones	stimulate	growth	of	body	hair	&	oil	glands	secrete	more	oil.		Skin	pores	become	clogged	&	bacteria	grow	in	clogged	pores.	• When	body	works	to	kill	the	bacteria,	whiteheads,	blackheads	&	pustules	form	in	these	areas.	• Stress,	birth	control	pills	&	not	enough	sleep	can	make	acne	worse	
Types	of	Acne	• Whiteheads:		Closed,	plugged	oil	glands	[Not	Broken]	• Blackheads:		Open,	plugged	oil	glands,	sometimes	filled	with	pus	[Broken,	exposed	to	air	à	oxidation	causes	darker	appearance]	• Pustules:		Red,	inflamed,	infected	plugged	oil	glands.		Sometimes	filled	with	pus.	• Some	pimples	may	be	painful.		In	severe	cases,	cysts	(large	fluid-filled	bumps)	may	develop	under	skin	• Some	adolescent	acne	will	heal	spontaneously	at	the	end	of	the	adolescent	period.		Other	Methods:	• Eat	a	light	diet	• Keep	Bowel	movements	regular	• Keep	optimistic	• Choose	right	contraceptive	pill	for	women	• Keep	oil	&	dirt	out	of	pores	&	reduce	inflammation	[Keep	skin	clean]	• WM:		Topically	apply	products	containing	benzoyl	peroxide.		If	not	effective	alone,	add	antibiotic	medications	[orally	and/or	topically]	à	Consult	with	MD	[Doesn’t	really	work	and	messes	up	GI	tract]

Gynecological	Disorders	Physiological	Characteristic	of	Females	 Therapeutic	Characteristics	of	Females	• Qi	and	Blood	[Especially	Blood]:		Everything	is	Qi	&	Blood	related,	even	milk	s Menstruation,	conception,	labor,	breast-feeding,	vaginal	discharge.	s Post	partum	mother:		when	breastfeeding,	don’t	menstruate	[Use	Qi	&	Blood	to	transform	into	milk	for	nursing	à	focus	on	major	task	of	nursing]	s Women's	good	health	depends	on	well-coordinated	regulation	and	harmony	of	Qi	and	Blood.			s Women	are	also	especially	liable	to	injury	the	source	of	Blood.	s Women’s	Foundation:	Blood	à	Often	give	Blood	tonics	to	women	[Dang	Gui	(angelica):		Tonifies	&	moves	blood]	s Always	check	the	status	of	women’s	Qi	&	Blood	• Extra	Meridians:		Chong,	Ren,	Du	and	Dai	s Anatomically,	Chong,	Ren	and	Du	originate	from	the	Uterus.		[All	start	from	same	place,	root	like	a	tree	in	the	middle	s Dai:	Restricts	the	Chong,	Ren	and	Du	by	encircling	the	waist	[Dai	Mai	is	in	middle	of	body	&	Controls	how	much	Qi	&	Blood	are	needed	by	upper	&	lower	body].		When	you	raise	your	arms,	all	12	meridians	go	up	&	down	except	Dai	Mai	[Very	special!]	Shares	points	[LV	13,	GB	26,	27,	28]	s Chong:		Sea	of	Blood	[Largest	meridian	(Pacific	Ocean),	located	in	center	of	the	body	like	a	cylinder	between	the	Ren	&	Du	à	goes	through	a	lot	of	organs	&	intersects	many	meridians].		Almost	equivalent	to	progesterone	in	WM	theory.		Some	herbs	have	a	progesterone-like	effect.	Holds	the	original	material.		Women:		Blood	Palace	(uterus),	Men:		Essence	Palace	(Dan	Tian).		Borrows	points	[KD	11	à	21,	ST	30,	REN	1,	7,	SP	4	s Ren:	dominates	the	uterus	and	fetus	[Yin	meeting	place:		all	yin	meridians	meet,	in	charge	of	yin	fluids].		Almost	equivalent	to	estrogen	in	WM	theory	because	it’s	the	Yin	&	controls	yin	fluids.		Some	herbs	act	like	estrogen.	s Du:	dominates	Yang	Qi	and	Yang	meridians.	[All	Yang	meridians	&	functions].		Acts	like	testosterone	à	tonify	male	hormones,	focus	on	Du	Mai	s These	4	extra	meridians	are	responsible	for	female	physiological	functions	(menstruation,	vaginal	discharge,	pregnancy,	labor	and	breast-feeding).	s Menstrual	cycle	[Corresponds	to	moon]	(99%	follow	these	guidelines,	but	1%	may	be	different)	§ Normal:	about	28	days	in	length	[between	25	to	35	days]	§ Usually	lasts	for	from	3-5	days	with	some	exceptional	cases	lasting	7	days.			§ Menstrual	volume:	normally	about	50-100	milliliters	∗ Some	exceptions:		some	women	never	menstruated	until	got	pregnant	§ Menstruation	gives	women	a	chance	for	monthly	detox	and	also	gives	a	good	indication	of	overall	health	• Three-Foot	Yin	meridians:		Spleen,	Liver	&	Kidney	s Qi	and	Blood	are	produced	and	maintained	by	the	Zang-Fu	organs,	especially	the	SP,	LV,	and	KD.	§ SP:		Where	Qi	&	Blood	produced	§ LV:		Control	emotions,	storage	of	Blood		∗ Women’s	emotions	are	controlled	by	LV	∗ LV	can	easily	get	stagnated,	especially	in	the	old	days	when	women	had	no	rights,	no	education,	were	controlled	by	parents	or	husband	à	women	get	stressed,	depressed,	etc.	∗ Best	herb	for	LV	Qi	Stagnation:		Xiang	Fu	[Xiao	Yao	San]	∗ Women	easily	influenced	by	hormones	∗ First	Trimester	&	postpartum	are	very	difficult	times	§ KD:		Controls	development	&	degeneration	[every	7	years]	∗ Age	14:	Menstruation,	21:	Pregnancy,	49:	Menopause	∗ KD	also	controls	Egg	quality		s Women’s	Fundamental	Zang	Organ:	Liver	(Controls,	Qi,	Blood	&	emotions)	§ Women’s	LV	easily	damaged:		LV	Qi	stagnation,	stasis,	damp	heat,	etc.	Pathological	Characteristic	of	Females	• Blood	and	Qi	are	easy	to	be	damaged	or	disturbed.	s Qi	xu,	Qi	stagnation	[PMS]		§ Birth	control	pills	can	also	cause	stagnation	and	even	cancer	§ Hormone	replacement	therapy	can	cause	cancer	s Blood	xu,	Blood	stagnation,		s Blood	coldness	[polar	bear	plunge	is	not	good],	Blood	heat	• Chong	and	Ren	disharmony	and	injury	are	the	most	frequently	seen	pathology	for	women. 	Dysmenorrhea	Primary	dysmenorrhea:	About	half	of	menstruating	women.		Causes:	• Contraction-causing	chemicals	[prostaglandins,	arachidonic	acid	à	induce	myometrium	(uterine	muscle)	contractions]	• Abnormal	nervous	system	response:		Some	women	may	have	autonomic	nervous	systems	that	are	overly	sensitive	to	menstrual	cycle	changes		s Autonomic	nervous	system	regulates	heart	rate,	blood	pressure	&	contains	pain	receptors	in	nerve	fibers	in	uterus	&	pelvic	area	• Abnormalities	in	the	arteries	in	the	uterus	s Doppler	ultrasounds	show	impaired	blood	flow	through	uterine	arteries	in	women	with	severe	dysmenorrhea	• Genetic	factors	may	play	critical	role	in	>50%	of	primary	dysmenorrhea	cases.			s Onset	usually	occurs	2-3	years	after	periods	have	started	s Pain	typically	develops	when	bleeding	starts	&	continues	for	32-48	hours	Secondary		• Menstrually	related	pain	that	accompanies	another	medical	or	physical	condition	[endometriosis,	IUD,	pelvic	inflammatory	disease	[PID],	uterine	fibroids,	miscarriage,	ectopic	pregnancy,	uterine	polyps,	cancer]		Risk	Factors	• Adolescence	&	Early	Puberty:		Start	menstruating	early	(<	11	yo)	à	Higher	risk	for	severe	pain,	longer	periods,	longer	menstrual	cycles.	s 20%-90%	of	teenagers	report	menstrual	pain,	15%	report	severe	pain	• Over/Underweight:		Underweight	has	higher	risk	for	frequent	menstrual	pain.		Overweight	women	have	twice	the	risk	for	having	severe,	prolonged	cramping.	• Smoking	&	Alcohol	use:		Smokers	have	50%	higher	risk	of	menstrual	pain.			s Higher	risk	of	dysmenorrhea	for	women	exposed	to	secondhand	smoke.			s Alcohol	consumption	may	prolong	cramps.	• Stress	• Chronic	Pelvic	Pain:		Common	causes:		Stress	&	Irritable	bowel	syndrome

Leukorrhagia	Western	Etiology	&	Pathology	• In	women	of	reproductive	age,	normal	physiologic	discharge	may	be	mistaken	for	an	infection.	• Normal	Discharge:		Milky	white	or	mucoid,	arising	from	cervix	or	vagina	as	an	epithelial	transudate.			• Colonization	of	vagina	by	Lactobacillus	sp.	keeps	pH	normal	[3.8	–	4.2]	(acidic)	à	preventing	overgrowth	of	bacteria	&	yeast.		Menstrual	blood,	certain	infections	or	semen	makes	vaginal	pH	more	alkaline.	• Causes	of	vulvar	hypersensitivity:		Hygiene	sprays,	perfumes,	menstrual	pads,	laundry	soaps,	bleaches,	fabric	softeners,	fabric	dyes,	synthetic	fibers,	bath-water	additives,	toilet	tissue	s Tight,	non-porous	undergarments	or	poor	hygiene	may	foster	fungal	&	bacterial	growth	s Occasionally,	sensitivity	to	spermicides,	vaginal	lubricants	or	creams,	latex	condoms,	or	diaphragms	can	cause	irritation,	which	may	be	confused	with	an	infection.		Frequent	douching	with	irritants	can	result	in	an	overgrowth	of	vaginal	pathogens	à	leading	to	infection.	
• Reproductive	years:		Vulvitis	is	usually	secondary	to	vaginal	infections	• Premenarchal	&	Postmenopausal:		vulvitis	commonly	occurs	alone	• After	menopause	[natural	or	from	oophorectomy,	radiation	or	chemotherapy]:		estrogen	deficiency	causes	thinning	of	vaginal	mucosa	è	More	vulnerable	to	trauma	&	infection.		Occasionally,	thinning	results	in	irritation	without	infection	(atrophic	vaginitis)	à	scanty	&	alkaline	discharge.	• Candida	infections	may	occur	in	diabetic	women,	women	taking	hormone	replacement	therapy	or	tamoxifen.		Bacterial	vaginosis	is	common,	except	in	women	confined	to	bed	(nursing	homes).		Western	Treatment	• Physiologic	discharge	can	be	annoying	due	to	soiled	clothing	&	the	feeling	of	wetness,	but	unless	itching,	irritation	or	malodor	is	present,	treatment	is	not	indicated.	• Acute	vulvitis:		Identify	&	eliminate	cause,	take	measures	to	reduce	irritation	[wear	loose,	absorbent	cotton	clothing	that	allows	air	circulation	&	keep	vulva	clean].		Avoid	soups.		Topical	corticosteroids	reduce	itching	&	are	indicated	when	infection	is	absent.		Oral	antihistamines	diminish	itching	&	cause	drowsiness,	assisting	in	sleep.	• Postmenopausal	patients	with	atrophic	vaginitis	are	treated	with	estrogen.		Infertility	Definition	• Failure	to	conceive	after	over	3	years	of	marriage	[or	trying]	even	though	both	partners	are	healthy	• Failure	to	conceive	for	over	3	years	after	last	delivery	• PTSD	=	KD	Qi	damaged	• Get	scared	&	wet	pants	à	KD	Qi	Damaged		Other	therapies	• Ear	Acupuncture	• Blood	letting	plus	cupping:		UB	32,	UB	40	for	blood	stasis	s Use	large	needle	or	3	edged	needle,	then	place	cup	on	area	• Herbs	s Nuan	Gong	Ren	Zi	Wan	s Placenta	pill	s Ge	Jie	Da	Bu	Wan	s Dang	Gui	Pian:		Emperor’s	Formula	§ For	blood	deficiency,	low	estrogen	s Jing	Gui	Shen	Qi	Wan:		KD	Yang	Xu	s Shi	Quan	Da	Bu	Wan	:		Qi	Blood	Yin	Yang	Xu	s Zhi	Bai	Di	Huang	Wan:		KD	yin	Xu	with	deficiency	heat	s Xiao	Yao	Wan	s Fu	Fang	Dan	Shen	Pain:		Blood	stasis	s Qing	Qi	Hua	Tan	Wan:	Phlegm	accumulation		Western	Perspective	Infertility:		A	couple	cannot	conceive	after	one	year	of	unprotected	intercourse	• Affects	about	1	of	5	couples	in	USA	• Etiologic	factors	s Sperm	disorders:		35%	of	couples	s Ovulatory	dysfunction:		20%	s Tubal	dysfunction:		30%	s Abnormal	cervical	mucus:		5%	s Unidentified	factors:		10%	• Diagnosis	&	treatment	require	thorough	assessment	of	both	partners.	• Financial	burden	&	time	commitment	for	diagnostic	&	therapeutic	infertility	procedures	can	cause	marital	strife.	• Failure	to	conceive	often	generates	frustration,	emotional	stress,	feelings	of	inadequacy,	anger,	guilt	&	resentment	à	Couples	counseling	&	psychological	support	are	important	adjuncts	to	treatment.		Despite	all	efforts,	some	couples	do	not	achieve	pregnancy.		They	should	be	counseled	about	when	to	stop	treatments	&	consider	adoption.	
Related	Tests	&	Procedures	• Semen	analysis:		Major	test	for	evaluating	male	infertility.		Performed	after	2-3	days	of	sexual	abstinence.		Sperm	count	varies,	so	examine	at	least	2-3	ejaculates	obtained	at	intervals	of	no	less	than	a	week.	s Normal	Results	§ Sperm	count:		>	20	million/mL	§ Sperm	motility	at	1	&	3	hours	>	50%	§ Sperm	Morphology:		>	60%	• Testicular	Biopsy:		Assesses	function	of	seminiferous	tubules	• Ovulation	Monitoring:			s Daily	measurements	of	basal	body	temperature	§ Only	can	predict	ovulation	period	within	2	days	[not	very	reliable]	§ Nadir	[lowest	point]	in	temperature	suggests	impending	ovulation	§ Postovulatory	period:		Temperature	é	>	0.5	C	(0.9F)	s Pelvic	ultrasound	monitoring:		More	reliable,	measures	ovarian	follicle	diameter	s Ovulation	predictor	kits:		Detect	an	increase	in	urinary	luteinizing	hormone	[LH]	excretion	24	to	36	hour	before	ovulation.	• Hysterosalpingogram:		Procedure	performed	by	fluoroscopy	2-5	days	after	cessation	of	menstrual	flow	to	determine	tubal	patency,	show	intrauterine	adhesions,	anomalies,	filling	defects	&	pelvic	adhesions.		If	intrauterine	abnormality	found,	following	procedures	can	be	performed:	s Hysteroscopy:	Hysteroscopic	lysis	of	adhesions	to	improve	fertility.	s Laparoscopy:		Further	evaluation	of	tubal	function	&	sometimes	simultaneous	fulguration	or	laser	ablation	of	pelvic	adhesions	• Post-Coital	Test:		Cervical	mucus	examined	after	intercourse,	including	mucus	receptivity	&	ability	of	sperm	to	survive	&	access	upper	reproductive	tract.		Assisted	Reproductive	Techniques	• Intrauterine	insemination	(IUI):	Involves	placing	sperm	inside	a	woman’s	uterus	to	facilitate	fertilization.	Goal:	increase	number	of	sperm	that	reach	fallopian	tubes	à	increase	the	chance	of	fertilization.		Provides	sperm	an	advantage	by	giving	it	a	head	start,	but	still	requires	a	sperm	to	reach	and	fertilize	the	egg	on	its	own.	Less	invasive	&	less	expensive	option	than	IVF.	• In	Vitro	Fertilization	[IVF]:		Used	for	patients	with	tubal	disease,	endometriosis,	ogliosperma,	sperm	antibodies,	unexplained	infertility.		Procedure	involves	ovarian	hyperstimulation,	oocyte	retrieval,	fertilization,	embryo	culture	&	embryo	transfer	to	uterus.		Despite	transfer	of	multiple	embryos	(often	3-4),	average	term	pregnancy	rate	is	20-25%	per	attempt.	• Gamete	Intrafallopian	Tube	Transfer	[GIFT]:		Used	for	women	who	have	normal	tubal	function	but	unexplained	infertility	or	endometriosis.		Multiple	oocytes	&	sperm	are	obtained	like	for	IVF	but	transferred	(laparoscopically	or	transvaginally	with	ultrasound	guidance)	to	distal	fallopian	tubes,	where	fertilization	occurs.		Success	rate:		25-35%	at	most	infertility	centers.	• Intrascytoplasmic	Sperm	Injection:		Primary	method	of	achieving	pregnancy	when	other	techniques	have	not	resulted	in	fertilization	or	when	semen	analysis	detects	marked	abnormalities.		Sperm	are	injected	into	oocyte,	bypassing	sperm	abnormalities	that	affect	attachment	to	or	penetration	into	oocyte.

Morning	Sickness	• Symptoms	including	nausea,	vomiting,	anorexia,	dizziness	during	early	pregnancy	[often	within	first	trimester	of	gestation]	• Severe	cases:			s Hyperemesis	gravidarum	s May	emaciate	the	pregnant	woman	&	lead	to	other	disorders	• Common	complaint	in	early	stage	of	pregnancy	&	usually	subsides	between	week	12-16		Other	Therapies	• Auricular:		LV,	ST,	SP,	Shen	Men,	Zero	• Herbs	s Xiang	Sha	Liu	Jun	Zi	Wan:		Tonify	SP/ST	&	Wake	up	spleen	s Xiao	Yao	Wan:		LV	Qi	Stagnation	s Bu	Zhong	Yi	Qi	Wan:		SP	Qi	Sinking	[Prolapse]	§ Good	Zhong	Qi:		Speak	very	well,	keep	organs	in	position,	habitual	miscarriage	s Ginseng	Royal	Jelly	s Ginger:		Sheng	Jiang	[Gan	Jiang	is	too	hot]	
Western	Perspective	• Hyperemesis	Gravidarum:		Uncontrollable	nausea	&	vomiting	during	pregnancy	that	results	in	dehydration	&	acidosis.		Distinct	from	ordinary	morning	sickness	with	nausea	&	vomiting.		Many	pregnant	women	with	morning	sickness	feel	that	they	are	vomiting	everything	they	ingest,	but	if	they	continue	to	gain	weight	&	aren’t	dehydrated,	they	do	not	have	hyperemesis	gravidarum.		Persistent	hyperemesis	gravidarum	is	uncommon	but	may	be	associated	with	serious	LV	damage.	• Treatment:		Acidosis	&	dehydration	corrected	with	IV	fluids,	gluose,	electrolytes.		Patient	should	be	kept	in	bed	in	a	hospital	&	not	given	anything	by	mouth	for	24	hours.		Anti-emetics	&	sedatives	used	as	necessary.		Occasionally,	IV	vitamin	therapy	necessary.

	Myopia:		Near-sightedness	or	close-sightedness	Definition	• Patient	can	clearly	see	objects	that	are	close	but	not	objects	that	are	far	away	(obscurely)	due	to	hypofunction	of	lens	flexibility.		Mostly	occurs	in	children	or	teenagers.	• Usually	can	be	completely	corrected	with	glasses,	contact	lenses,	surgery	• TCM:		Disorder	called	“Able	to	see	the	near	&	scared	to	look	at	distant.”		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Occurs	when	eye	is	too	long	for	focus	point	of	cornea.	• Light	rays	that	normally	focus	on	the	retina	focus	in	front	of	it.	• Causes	images	to	appear	blurry.	• Some	experts	think	that	myopia	is	something	that	you’re	born	with.	• Some	think	it’s	caused	by	the	stress	of	too	much	reading,	close	work	or	computer	use.	• It’s	likely	that	both	factors	play	a	role	in	this	disorder.	• Cornea	that	is	too	steep	may	also	cause	myopia.	• In	older	age,	cataracts	can	cause	myopia.	s Long	standing	nearsighted	work	s Inappropriate	working	posture	s Working	in	dim	conditions	s Insufficient	LV	&	KD:		Essence	deficiency,	Yin/Yang	deficiency		Differentiation	• Family	History	• Eye	Health	• Blurred	vision	in	looking	at	objects,	especially	distant	objects	• Eye	discomfort	• Eye	strain		Diagnosis	• Eye	doctor	will	check	patient	by	Reading	letters	or	numbers	on	a	chart	20	feet	away,	to	measure	the	ability	to	see	distant	objects	clearly	s Dilate	eyes	by	putting	eyedrops	in	them,	then	look	at	retinas	through	opthalmoscope	[look	through	pupil]	s Have	patient	look	through	various	lenses	like	those	in	glasses	to	see	which	ones	give	patient	the	best	distance	vision	
Treatment	Principle	• Regulate	the	Qi	in	the	meridians	around	the	eyes,	nourish	LV	&	KD		Prescriptions	• Points	around	eyes:		ST	1,	UB	1	s Techniques:		Needle	slowly	with	no	rotation	s Close	needle	hole	&	press	for	at	least	2	minutes	upon	withdrawal	• Points:		GB	20,	Yi	Ming,	Tai	Yang,	LI	4,	GB	37	s Yi	Ming	[Eye	Brightening]:		1	cun	posterior	to	SJ	17	s Techniques:		Moderate	stimulation	to	obtain	Qi	extending	as	far	as	the	eye	region	or	forehead	s Conduct	Qi	from	distal	points	into	eyeball	s Point	needle	tip	towards	eye	• Secondary	Points	s KD	Deficiency:		KD	3,	7,	9	[Beautiful	Baby],	UB	23,	UB	52	s Nourish	LV:		LV	8	[nourish],	SP	6	[Good	Combo]	s Auricular:		Shen	Men,	Zero,	Occiput,	Forehead,	LV,	KD	• SI	6	[Yang	Lao	à	Take	care	of	elders]:		Good	point,	secondary	point	for	deficiency	caused	eye	disorders		Other	Therapies	• Ear	Acupuncture	• Seven	Star	Needle:		Back	Shu,	HTJJ	[Neck	area]	• Tui	Na:		Around	eye	à	GB	20	• Herbs	s Loose	Herbs	§ Chrysanthemum	[good	for	eye,	especially	wind	heat],		§ Lycium	fruit	[Gou	Qi	Zi:		Goji	Berry	à	nourish	LV	Blood],		§ Equisetum	[Horse	Tail	(Mu	Zei)]	s Patent	§ Jin	Shi	Ming	[Myopia,	especially	for	kids]	§ Ming	Mu	Di	Huang	Wan:		Traditional	formula	KD	&	LV	tonic	§ Myopia	Pills:		Often	for	kids,	mild	cases,	when	first	diagnosed	§ Shi	Hu	Ye	Guang	Wan:		Traditional	formula	• Acupuncture	definitely	effective	in	treating	non-congenital	myopia	s Tui	Ni	2-3	times	a	day	around	eyes,	whenever	you	feel	tired		Western	Treatment	• Corrective	Lenses:		Can	help	eyeball	to	focus	light	rays	properly	on	retina	• Laser	Photorefractive	Keratectomy	[PRK]:		Laser	removes	tissue	from	surface	of	cornea

Conjunctivitis	Definition	• Inflammation	of	conjunctiva	(clear	membrane	that	lines	the	eyelids	&	covers	white	of	eye)	[Pink/Red,	Fire	eyes]	• TCM:		Common	acute	condition	caused	by	wind	heat	invasion	or	LV	&	GB	fire	s TCM	Name:		Red	Eye,	Fire	Eye	• Commonly	seen	external	eye	disorder	• Common	Causes:			s Overindulgence	in	alcohol,	overuse	of	eyes,		s Exposure	to	wind	heat,	lack	of	light,		s Inadequate	sleep,	graveyard	shift,	thinking/worrying	too	much	at	night	s Allergies	[wind	heat	or	wind	damp	heat]	s Angry	people	à	constantly	yelling	à	LV	congested,	LV	fire,	toxins	à	Red	eyes		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Many	Causes:		Bacterial	or	viral	infections	s Viruses:		Many	that	cause	the	common	cold	s Bacteria:		Chlamydia,	staphylococci,	streptococci	• Viral	&	Bacterial	causes	of	conjunctivitis	can	spread	easily,	by	coughing,	sneezing	or	contact	with	contaminated	objects	[hands,	washcloths,	towels,	cosmetics,	false	eyelashes,	soft	contact	lenses]	• Don’t	rub	or	touch	eyes!!!	• LU:		LU	in	charge	of	skin.		Conjunctiva	is	transparent	skin	on	top	of	eye	à	wind	heat,	damp	invasion	s Wind	Heat	• LV	&	GB	Fire:		Get	angry,	eat	too	much	spicy	food	• Yin	or	fluids	deficiency:		Scratchy,	dry	eyes	[like	to	rub,	stimulate	tears],	dry	eye	syndrome		Differentiation		• Sudden	onset	[Wind	Heat,	Drink	too	much,	Yelling	at	people]	• Congestion,	swelling	&	pain	of	eyelids,	photophobia,	lacrimation,	watery	or	sticky	eye	discharge	• Wind	Heat:		Itchy	or	scratchy	eyes,	redness	[Fever	&	chills,	more	fever	than	chills,	sore	throat,	cough,	P:		Floating,	rapid]	s Happens	very	quickly	• LV	&	GB	Fire:		Pronounced	redness,	pus	discharge,	mood	swings		s Fire	eye:		bloodshot,	veins	broken	(looks	awful)	s P:		Wiry	&	rapid,	T:		Very	red,	especially	sides	• Viral	conjunctivitis	usually	gets	worse	5-7	days	after	onset.		Can	improve	in	10	days	to	1	month.		If	only	one	eye	affected	at	first,	may	take	up	to	2	weeks	for	the	other	eye	to	be	affected.		If	both	eyes	affected,	first	eye	has	worse	conjunctivitis	than	second.	[Pus:		dead	tissue,	white	blood	cells]	s Use	acupuncture	&	herbs	to	shorten	s Da	Qing	Ye	very	good	for	viruses	• Bacterial	conjuctivits:		Should	improve	within	2-3	days	of	antibiotic	application	[Eyedrops	better	than	oral	antibiotics]	s Huang	Lian	very	good	for	bacteria	s If	use	herbs,	use	concentrated	forms	to	get	effects	s In	China:	Jin	Yin	Hua,	Chai	Hu:		Inject	into	IV	or	intramuscular	injections	§ Drops	in	nose	for	kids:		Reduces	fever	very	quickly	
Treatment	Principle	• Disperse	wind,	drain	heat	&	fire	• Use	distal	&	local	points	in	combination	with	reducing	techniques		Prescriptions	• Tai	Yang,	LI	4,	GB	20,	UB	2	• Experienced	Points:		GB	8,	UB	1	s Very	good	points	• Secondary	Points	s Wind	Heat:		SJ	5,	LU	11,	DU	23	s LV	GB	Fire:		LV	2,	GB	43	s Headaches:		Yin	Tang,	GB	14	s Irritability,	Feverish:		SJ	1		Other	therapies	• Ear	acupuncture:		LV,	GB,	LU,	Eyes,	Ear	Apex	• Bloodletting	[Only	for	acute,	very	severe	cases]		s UB	2,	Tai	Yang,	Yin	Tang	[3-5	drops	blood]:			s Just	use	regular	needles,	blot	with	dry	cotton	ball	s Must	use	gloves!		Don’t	do	every	day,	just	once	• Moxa:		2	extra	points	[Best	to	use	real	moxa	à	3-5	units]	s Da	Gu	Kong:		On	thumb	[nail	side,	middle	of	distal	joint]	s Shao	Gu	Kong:		On	pinky	[nail	side,	middle	of	proximal	joint]	• Herbs	[Clear	Heat]	s Ju	Hua	s Man	Jing	Zi	s Jue	Ming	Zi	s Qing	Xiang	Zi	s Mu	Ze	s Patent:				§ Yin	Qiao	[wind	heat]	§ LV/GB	Fire:		Long	Dan	Xie	Gan	Wan		Western	Treatment	• Viral	Conjunctivits:		Antihistamine	pills	to	relieve	itching	&	redness	s Eyedrops	can	help	control	symptoms	• Bacterial	Conjunctivitis:		Antibiotic	eyedrops	• Wash	eyes	gently	to	remove	any	pus	or	crusts,	dry	gently	with	clean	towel	• Patients	who	wear	contact	lenses	should	stop	wearing	until	eyes	healed.		Prevention	• Wash	hands	frequently.		Do	not	touch	or	rub	eyes	• Never	share	eye	makeup	or	cosmetics	with	anyone	• Never	use	eye	medicine	that	has	been	prescribed	for	anyone	else	• Do	not	share	towels,	washcloths	or	sheets	with	anyone	• Avoid	swimming	in	swimming	pools	if	you	have	conjunctivitis.

THERAPEUTICS	6:		EYES,	EARS,	NOSE,	THROAT	[EENT]	
	 110	
Glaucoma	• Definition:		An	eye	disease	in	which	the	flow	of	aqueous	humor	(clear,	watery	fluid	that	normally	circulates	in	eye)	is	out	of	balance,	with	more	fluid	staying	in	the	eye	than	exiting	due	to	flow	of	fluid	out	of	eye	becomes	blocked.		Results	in	painful	buildup	of	fluid	in	eyeball	that	creates	pressure,	leading	to	impaired	vision.		Patients	often	report	seeing	halos	around	lights	in	earlier	stages.		If	untreated,	the	increased	pressure	can	cause	atrophy	of	the	optic	nerve	and	cause	blindness.	• TCM	Name:		Qing	Feng	Nei	Zhang	[Green	Wind	Internal	Blockage]	• Main	Organs:		LV,	KD,	SP		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Onset	can	be	rapid	&	acute	or	slow	&	chronic	• Two	main	types,	depending	on	how	flow	of	fluid	is	blocked	• Open	Angle	Glaucoma:		Caused	by	gradual	blockage	in	area	that	drains	fluid	from	eyes	into	drainage	channels.		More	common	type	of	glaucoma	in	US	&	Europe.		Usually	affects	both	eyes.	• Closed	Angle	Glaucoma:		(More	dangerous,	often	because	of	injury	or	trauma)	Blockage	cause	by	a	change	in	the	position	of	the	iris	(colored	part	of	eye),	which	causes	the	iris	to	block	the	drainage	channels.		Usually	happens	in	one	eye	at	a	time.		If	one	eye	gets	glaucoma,	the	other	eye	is	at	risk	for	having	the	same	problem.		When	this	type	of	glaucoma	happens	suddenly,	it	is	called	acute	closed-angle	glaucoma	&	is	a	medical	emergency.			s Symptoms	of	acute	closed	angle	glaucoma	s Severe	eye	pain,	Seeing	halos	around	lights	s Nausea	&	vomiting,	Headache,	Loss	of	vision		Differentiation	• Normal	IOP	[Intraocular	pressure]:		11-21	mm/Hg	• Exceptions:	s Normal	or	low	pressure	glaucoma:		Some	people	with	normal	pressure	develop	optic	nerve	damage		s Ocular	hypertension:	Patients	have	pressure	>	21	mm/Hg	without	any	optic	nerve	injury	• Open	angle	&	other	chronic	forms	of	glaucoma	often	have	no	symptoms	in	the	early	stages.		In	later	stages,	patients	will	begin	to	notice	a	loss	of	vision	[side	vision	is	affected	first]	• Glaucoma	tends	to	run	in	families.		Occurs	most	often	after	age	35,	but	sometimes	children	have	it.		Common	eye	disorder	in	people	over	age	60.	• In	US,	more	common	in	black	people	than	white	people	[most	likely	because	of	eye	structure]

	Lid	Abscess	[Sty]	• Definition:		Also	named	meibomianitis	(acute	inflammation	of	sebaceous	glands	at	edge	of	eyelids)	• TCM	Name:		Needle	eye,	wheat	abscess	[Due	to	shape	&	appearance]		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Sty	occurs	when	bacteria	infect	one	of	the	tiny	glands	at	the	base	of	eyelid	hairs	à	gland	becomes	inflamed		Differentiation	• At	first,	symptoms	only	local	redness	&	swelling	in	area	of	sty.		• Often	a	tiny	&	hard	pimple	present	at	affected	lid.		Infection	usually	painful	&	tender	only	if	touched.		Soon	infection	gets	worse,	redder,	bigger,	very	tender	with	pressure,	pain	aggravated	with	head	down.	• 3-4	days	after	onset,	yellow	purulent	dot	appears	at	tip	of	pimple,	then	infection	bursts	&	drains	(pus	flows	out).		Don’t	affect	vision	unless	swelling	blocks	vision.	• Very	safe	as	long	as	you	don’t	cause	a	big	infection	[Don’t	squeeze	à	Get	very	swollen	eyelid]

Cataracts	• Definition:		Lens	condition	affecting	eye	in	which	lens	or	lens	capsule	becomes	opaque,	resulting	in	blurry	or	dim	vision	• Usually	affects	seniors	&	thought	in	most	cases	to	be	a	natural	part	of	aging	process.		Few	people	over	age	60	don’t	have	beginning	of	a	cataract.	• In	most	people,	cataract	never	develops	far	&	may	be	little	more	than	a	small	“milky”	dot	in	part	of	the	lens.		In	others,	this	milkiness	can	extend	to	whole	lens	[but	not	necessarily].		Opacity	gradually	becoming	worse	until	lens	resembles	a	hard-boiled	egg.	• Western	Medicine:		treat	cataracts	by	removing	them	surgically	when	condition	progresses	enough	to	warrant	it.	• Prevention:		Move	&	exercise	eye	muscles!!!	• TCM	Name:		Yuan	Yi	Nei	Zhang	[Internal	Screen	in	Eye]		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Main	Organs:		KD,	LV,	SP,	LU	• Body	fluids:		Quality	&	quantity	keeps	Lens	clear	&	transparent	s When	body	fluids	become	“turbid”	or	stagnant,	cataracts	will	gradually	develop.		Lung	is	responsible	for	circulation	of	body	fluids	&	includes	circulation	inside	eye.	s KD	Yin	Xu	or	exhaustion:		Body	fluids	governed	by	kidneys	&	can	become	turbid	[yin	fails	to	nourish	eye]	s LV	Blood	Xu:		Fails	to	nourish	eye	or	LV	Heat	rises	[worse]	&	disturbs	clear	orifices	• SP	Qi	Xu:				Western	View	• Free	radicals:		Lens	gradually	becomes	more	opaque	as	proteins	that	form	lens	degrade.		Process	occurs	naturally	with	time	&	is	somewhat	analogous	to	degradation	of	plastics	in	sunlight.		The	process	accelerated	by	presence	of	free	radicals	in	blood.		Many	sources	of	free	radicals:		Imperfect	digestion	of	some	proteins,	factory	produced	food,	margarine	&	canola	oil	intake,	UV	light,	cigarette	smoke,	photosensitizing	drugs	(gout	medications,	cholesterol	lowering	drugs,	antibiotics,	diuretics),	steroids	• Diabetes:		Western	mechanism	unknown	s TCM:		Yin	deficiency	[Xiao	Ke	(drink,	eat,	pee	a	lot,	never	gain	weight)	• Over/Under-exposure	to	light	[too	bright,	too	dim]	
Differentiation	• Early	stages:		Cataract	often	starts	as	a	localized	cloudy	area.		At	first,	there	may	be	1	or	2	diffuse	spots	that	may	be	off-center	and	vision	may	be	affected	except	in	poor	light	when	the	pupil	is	fully	dilated.	• Gradually,	these	spots	grow	in	size	&	opacity.		Patients	initially	may	be	unaware	of	a	cataract	developing.		They	may	perceive	halos	around	objects	or	things	may	become	blurred.		Little	by	little,	vision	deteriorates	so	that	is	more	&	more	difficult	to	read	&	discern	objects.	• In	addition,	patients	can	develop	a	so-called	“second	sight,”	where	eyesight	improves	for	short	interval	but	does	not	last.	• Food:	s Red	food:		Gou	JI	Zi,	Da	Zao,	Red	beans,	beets,	liver		Technique	• Local	points	needled	gently	with	enough	stimulation	to	feel	Qi	in	front	of	eye	being	treated.	• Near	points	needled	on	side	of	affected	eye,	toward	eye	• If	condition	is	bilateral,	then	needle	both	sides	• Tonifying	techniques	applied	for	Xu	types.	• Reducing	ones	for	Shi	types.	• Tonifying	with	reducing	or	Reducing	with	tonifying	for	Xu	&	Shi	types.		Other	Therapies	• Seven	Star	needling:		GB	20,	HTJJ	at	C	level,	UB	15,	UB	18,	UB	23,	SP	6	s Tonifying	techniques.	• Auricular:		LV,	KD,	Eye,	Brain,	SP,	Shen	Men	s Gold:		More	tonifying	s Silver:		More	reducing		Prognosis	• Most	significant	progress	with	early	stage	cataracts	• If	cataracts	has	only	just	started,	there	is	a	possibility	of	reversing	it	completely	• Once	cataract	has	spread	&	opacity	has	deepened,	chances	of	complete	reversal	are	slim.	• When	patient	is	old,	progress	will	be	slow	&	frequent	treatments	are	expected.	• It	should	be	possible	to	prevent	cataract	from	getting	worse,	but	regular	maintenance	treatment	needed. Strabismus	Definition	• Strabismus:		Also	named	squint,	refers	to	disorder	where	the	two	eyes	of	patient	area	unable	to	stare	straight	simultaneously	[eyes	point	in	different	directions.]		Usually	one	eye	pointed	straight	ahead	&	other	pointed	in,	out,	up	or	down.			• TCM:		Wind	blowing	squint		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Normal	Vision:		Brain	fuses	images	from	two	eyes	aimed	at	same	target	into	one	clear	image	[called	binocular	vision].	• Strabismus:		Two	different	images	sent	to	brain.		Occurs	when	eye	muscles	are	not	balanced.		Movements	of	muscles	of	one	eye	do	not	match	those	of	other.	s Young	child:	brain	starts	to	ignore	image	from	turned	eye	&	child	loses	depth	perception	&	vision	in	turned	eye.	s Adults:		Often	have	double	vision	because	the	brain	is	used	to	receiving	two	images	and	cannot	easily	ignore	image	from	turned	eye.		Causes	• LV	&	KD	Deficiency	• Wind	invasion	into	meridians	around	eye:	Internal	wind	from	high	fever	• Inadequate	gesture	• Congenital	strabismus	• Trauma	• Paralytic	Strabismus:		Muscular	paralysis	around	eye	s Estropia	[Eye	turns	IN]	(Cross-eye):	Abductor	paralysis	[internal	strabismus]	s Extropia	Eye	turns	OUT	(walleye):	Adductor	paralysis	[external	strabismus]	s Hypertropia:	Inferior	muscle	paralysis,	Eye	turns	UP	s Hypotropia:	Superior	muscle	paralysis,	Eye	turns	DOWN	• Conjugate	Strabismus:		Most	Difficult	to	treat		Differentiation	• All:		Difficult	movement	• Squinting	in	one	or	both	eyes,	turning	or	tilting	head,	limited	movement	of	one	or	both	eyes,	diplopia		Treatment	Principles	• Regulate	muscles	of	eyes	• Tonify	LV	&	KD		Point	Prescription	• Points:		ST	2,	ST	36,	LI	4,	GB	20	• Internal	[lateral	weak	muscles]:			s Tai	Yang,	GB1,	SJ	23,	Qiu	Hou	[stimulation	on	lateral	side]	• External	[medial	weak	muscles]:			s UB	1,	UB	2,	Yu	Yao,	Shang	Jing	Ming,	Xia	Jing	Ming		Other	Therapies	• Electroacupuncture	s Tai	Yang,	distal	points,	same	side	as	points	s Mild	stimulation	s Don’t	retain	needles	• Tonifying	effect	[muscle	deficiency	à	Strengthen	muscles]	
		Western	View	&	Treatment	Strabismus	in	adults	may	be	caused	by:	• Strabismus	from	childhood	that	recurs	• Poor	vision	in	one	eye,	which	begins	to	drift	outward	• Concussion	(injury	to	brain)	• Injury	to	nerves	that	control	eye	muscle	movement,	sometimes	as	a	result	of	high	blood	pressure,	thyroid	disorders,	diabetes,	myasthenia	gravis,	MS,	inflammatory	&	infectious	diseases,	tumors,	aneurysms	Diagnosis:		Eye	doctor	tests	vision	&	ability	to	follow	objects	with	each	eye	Treatment:		Goals:		Restore	vision,	if	amblyopia	present,	straighten	eyes	&	restore	the	ability	to	make	normal	eye	contact	&	restore	binocular	vision.	• In	children,	treatment	should	begin	as	soon	as	the	condition	is	detected.		Treatment	that	begins	after	6	may	improve	a	child’s	appearance	but	not	always	the	vision.	• Treating	any	amblyopia	by	patching	or	using	special	eyedrops	in	the	good	eye,	which	force	the	brain	to	pay	attention	to	the	weak	eye.	• Glasses	for	farsightedness	or	to	improve	the	focus	of	the	eye.		Farsighted	children	have	to	work	harder	to	focus	their	eyes.		[An	eye	with	poor	vision	is	more	likely	to	drift.]		Glasses	that	correct	the	farsightedness	help	keep	the	eyes	from	crossing	while	they	are	worn	• Eye	exercises	to	train	the	eyes	to	move	together	&	focus	on	the	same	object	at	the	same	time.	• Prism	glasses	for	adults,	which	may	eliminate	double	vision	while	they	are	being	worn.	• Surgery	on	the	eye	muscles.		Muscles	may	be	loosened,	tightened,	or	repositioned.		If	this	surgery	is	performed	early	enough	in	some	children,	they	may	develop	normal	vision.		Like	any	surgery,	eye	surgery	has	risks.		Surgery	success	depends	partly	on	whether	coordination	between	the	eyes	&	brain	is	good	enough	to	keep	the	eyes	locked	on	target	&	in	proper	alignment.				 Wind	Invasion	 LV	&	KD	Deficiency	 Paralytic	Strabismus	 Conjugate	Strabismus	Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	Wind	invasion	into	meridians	around	eye:			• Usually	happens	during	high	fever	[like	meningitis],	infection	may	spread	out	in	blood	very	quickly	à	systemic	infection,	septicemia.		When	blood	is	infected,	it	moves	very	fast	à	may	shake	up	things	inside.		In	small	arteries,	clots	may	block.	• Extreme	heat	à	internal	wind	
• Gradual	onset	with	dizziness,	blurred	vision,	tinnitus,		• Deep,	thready	pulse	 • Muscular	paralysis	around	eye	[Or	nerve	that	control	muscles]	• 	 • Muscular	imbalance	• Sometimes	the	cause	of	eye	muscle	imbalance	not	known	• Children	may	be	born	without	ability	to	keep	the	eyes	together	• Children	with	conditions	like	cerebral	palsy	or	Down	syndrome	often	develop	strabismus	• Difficult	to	treat	Symptoms	• Sudden	onset	with	headache,		• Nausea,	vomiting,	fever	&	chills	• Floating,	rapid	pulse	 • Gradual	onset	with	dizziness,		• Blurred	vision,	tinnitus,		• Deep,	thready	pulse	 • Patient	usually	has	diploplia	[seeing	double],		• Dizziness,	nausea,	vomiting	• Have	big	difference	in	vision	between	the	two	eyes		• [20/20	&	20/40,	etc.]

Optic	Nerve	Atrophy	• Optic	Nerve	Atrophy:		Chronic	eye	disorder	characterized	by	gradual	degeneration	of	visual	acuity.			s Initial	Stage:		Blurry	vision	s Eyesight	gradually	&	continuously	decreases	• TCM	Name:		Green	Blindness	s Mainly	caused	by	Qi	&	Blood	deficiency		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Western:		Glaucoma,	tumor,	embolism	or	trauma	can	lead	to	malnutrition	of	optic	nerve,	resulting	in	atrophy	of	optic	nerve.	• Divided	into	primary	&	secondary	s Primary:		Due	to	pathology	distant	from	nerve	head	§ Optic	disk	become	white	or	grayish	with	sharp	edges	s Secondary:		Due	to	pathology	at	nerve	head	§ Optic	disk	becomes	dirty	white	with	irregular		Differentiation	• Visual	loss:		Roughly	proportional	to	degree	of	nerve	atrophy	s Ranges	from	little	loss	of	vision	to	total	blindness	with	no	papillary	response	to	direct	light.	• Eye	appears	normal	but	eyesight	keeps	decreasing	s At	first,	blurred	vision	(green,	blue	or	yellow	in	color)	s Eyesight	keeps	decreasing	à	Visual	field	reduces	à	Optic	papilla	turns	pale	à	Blindness	[Last]		Western	View	• Optic	Atrophy	is	a	sign	of	chronic	optic	nerve	disease	&	not	a	standalone	diagnosis	à	its	presence	demands	a	search	for	a	cause.	• Dramatic	return	of	vision	can	accompany	reversal	of	certain	pathologic	processes,	for	example,	central	vision	&	visual	field	defects	may	return	after	relief	of	pressure	on	the	optic	nerve	caused	by	a	tumor.

Ear	Infection	Definition	• Ear	Infection	(otitis	media):	acute	or	chronic	inflammation	of	auris	media	(middle	ear).			• Most	common	form	of	ear	infection,	seen	mostly	in	children,	characterized	by	otopyorrhea	(excessive	ear	discharge)	&	ear	pain.			• TCM	Name:		Ting	Er	[purulent	ear]		Etiology	&	Pathology	• Shao	Yang	Meridians	mainly	involved	in	ear	infection	s Hand	Shao	Yang	Meridian	[SJ]	enters	ear	from	back	à	ends	at	SJ	21	s Foot	Shao	Yang	Meridian	[GB]	enters	ear	from	back	à	ends	at	GB	2		Other	Therapies	• Auricular:		Ear,	SJ,	GB	[excess],	KD	[deficiency]	• Bloodletting:		Bleed	Ear	apex	for	excess	• Tui	Na:		Back	of	ear,	front	of	throat,	nape	of	neck,	cervical	HTJJ	à	Pinch	• Herbs	s Yin	Chiao	Jie	Du	Wan	s Long	Dan	Xie	Gan	Wan	s Zhi	Bai	Di	Huang	Wan	s Zuo	Gui	Wan	
Prevention	• Avoid	pacifier	use	• Prevent	viral	influenza	• Prevent	bacterial	infections	• Breastfeeding	• Prevent	colds/flu	• Avoid	exposure	to	cigarette	smoke	• Dietary	factors	&	supplements		Western	View	• Ear:		Organ	of	hearing	&	balance	• Organized	into	External,	middle	&	internal	areas	s Outer	Ear:		collects	sound	waves	à	conducted	through	canal	to	tympanic	membrane	[ear	drum]	s Tympanic	Membrane:		Tissue	lined	on	the	inside	with	mucus.		Like	a	drum,	vibrates	to	the	incoming	sound	waves,	converting	them	into	mechanical	energy.	s Middle	Ear:		Complex	structure	filled	with	air	&	composed	of	tiny	bones	that	vibrate	to	the	rhythm	of	the	mechanical	energy	that	resonates	through	the	eardrum.		Passes	sound	waves	on	to	the	inner	ear.	s Inner	Ear:		Filled	with	fluid.		Hair-like	structures	stimulate	nerves	to	convert	the	mechanical	waves	to	electrochemical	impulses	that	are	carried	by	a	network	of	nerve	cells	to	the	brain,	which	senses	the	impulses	as	sounds.		Also	contains	3	semi-circular	canals	that	function	as	body’s	gyroscope,	regulating	balance.	s Eustachian	Tube:		Important	structure	in	ear,	runs	from	middle	ear	to	passages	behind	nose	&	upper	part	of	throat.		Ventilates	ear	&	equalizes	air	pressure	in	middle	ear	to	outside	air	pressure.		Problems	here	are	primary	factors	in	most	ear	infections.			 Acute	[Wind	Heat	Invasion	or	GB	Damp	Heat]	 Chronic	[Yin	Fluid	Deficiency]	Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Shao	Yang	Meridians	[SJ,	GB]:		Govern	Excess	ear	disorders	s Sudden	hearing	loss	à	Excess	[LV	Yang	rising,	LV	Fire,	LV	Wind	blow	phlegm	to	ear	&	block	it]	• Wind	heat	invasion:		Sore	throat,	URI	à	middle	ear	infection	due	to	Eustachian	tube	blockage	• GB	Damp	Heat	causing	upward	disturbance:		More	common	in	adults	[LV	Yang	rising]	
• KD:		Relates	to	ear	with	deficiency,	chronic	disorders	[aging,	dehydration]	• Yin	fluid	deficiency:		leading	to	inadequate	nourishment	[aging]	
Symptoms	• Sudden	onset	[infection,	anger]	• Throbbing	pain	in	ear,	impaired	hearing	• Possible	fever,	chills,	headache,	dizziness	• Coughing,	nasal	congestion,	irritability,	loss	of	appetite,	vomiting	• Possible	yellow	purulent	discharge	within	a	few	days	of	onset	due	to	eardrum	rupture	
• Lingering	or	recurrent	attacks	of	otitis	media	• Continuous	scanty	purulent	discharge	from	ear	• Dizziness,	impaired	hearing,	tinnitus,		• Exacerbation	by	cold/flu	or	swimming	• Possible	lassitude,	mild	fever	• Not	painful,	feeling	of	stuffiness	in	ears,	like	being	underwater	Tongue	• Red	with	yellow	coating	 • Pale,	dry		Pulse	• Rapid,	floating,	slippery	 • Deep,	thready	Children’s		Symptoms	Acute	Otitis	Media	[AOM]:		Infection	in	middle	ear	that	causes	inflammation	behind	tympanic	membrane.		Often	develops	during	or	after	cold	or	flu.		Extremely	common	in	children.	• Difficult	to	determine	if	a	pre-verbal	child	or	infant	has	ear	infection.	• Some	believe	that	tugging	on	ear	can	indicate	infection,	but	more	likely	indicates	teething	pain.	• Some	children	may	indicate	pain	if	they	have	trouble	swallowing		• Severe:		Tympanic	membrane	may	rupture	&	discharge	pus	[usually	brings	relief	from	pain]	• Fever	&	chills	make	children	irritable	&	fussy	à	difficult	to	determine	if	otitis	media	is	also	present	• 1/3	of	children:		Symptoms	not	apparent	
Otitis	Media	with	Effusion	[OME]:		Effusion	(fluid)	builds	up	in	one	or	both	middle	ears.		When	this	is	chronic	&	severe,	the	fluid	is	very	sticky	&	commonly	called	“glue	ear.”	• Only	signal	to	parent	that	condition	exists	may	be	child	complains	of	“plugged	up”	hearing	• Other	symptoms:		not	responding	to	verbal	commands,	talking	louder,	turning	up	TV	or	radio	• Children	susceptible	to	OME	can	have	frequent	episodes	for	more	than	half	of	their	first	3	years	of	life.	• Episodes	can	last	from	weeks	to	months

Tinnitus	&	Deafness	Definition	• Tinnitus	&	Deafness:		Auditory	disorders	• Tinnitus:		Characterized	by	ringing	sound	in	ears	felt	by	patient	[Perception	of	sound	in	absence	of	acoustic	stimulus	(subjective	experience	of	patient)].		Usually	an	omen	of	deafness.		Discussed	together	because	of		s TCM	Name:		Er	Ming	• Deafness:		Failing	or	loss	of	hearing.	s TCM	Name:		Er	Long	• Keep	KD	strong	à	Don’t	lose	hearing	• Zang	Fu:		KD	(Xu),	Excess:		Shao	Yang	(SJ,	GB)		Western	View:		Mechanism	causing	tinnitus	is	obscure	• May	occur	as	a	symptom	of	nearly	all	ear	disorders	à	Ear	canal	obstruction,	infections,	Eustachian	tube	obstruction,	Meniere’s	Disease,	ototoxicity,	cardiovascular	diseases	(hypertension,	arteriosclerosis,	aneurysms),	anemia,	noise-induced	hearing	loss,	acoustic	trauma,	ringing	at	same	rate	as	heartbeat	(hyperthyroidism,	hypertension,	aneurysms),	anemia,	noise-induced	hearing	loss,	acoustic	trauma	(blast	injury,	head	trauma,	etc.)		Treatment	• The	ability	to	tolerate	tinnitus	varies	by	patient.	• Treatment	should	target	the	underlying	disease.	• Correcting	associated	hearing	loss	usually	relieves	the	tinnitus.	• Hearing	aids	may	suppress	tinnitus.			• No	specific	medical	or	surgical	therapy	for	tinnitus,	but	many	patients	find	relief	by	playing	background	music	to	mask	the	tinnitus	&	may	go	to	sleep	with	the	radio	playing.	• Some	patients	benefit	from	using	a	tinnitus	masker,	a	device	worn	like	a	hearing	aid	that	presents	a	sound	more	pleasant	than	the	tinnitus.	• Hearing	Aids:		Don’t	restore	hearing,	just	make	sounds	louder	to	get	best	sound	possible	to	patient’s	ear.		May	distort	some	sounds.	• Cochlear	Implants:		Cochlea	is	part	of	ear	that	turns	vibrations	into	electrical	signals.		Help	with	speech	reading	by	providing	information	about	word	intonation,	speech	rhythm&	some	speech	perception.		Types	of	Hearing	Loss	• Conductive:		Hearing	loss	results	when	something	interferes	with	sound	waves	traveling	through	outer	&	middle	parts	of	ear.		Causes:	s Complete	blockage	in	outer	ear	by	wax,	infection	(otitis	media)	s Collection	of	fluid	in	middle	ear,	damage	to	tiny	bones	in	middle	ear	• Sensorineural:		Results	from	a	problem	in	the	innermost	part	of	the	ear	or	auditory	nerve.		Causes:	s Tumors,	injury	from	diseases	(meningitis,	rubella)	s Physical	injury	to	inner	ear,	abnormal	development	of	inner	ear	s Children	with	both	hearing	&	balance	problems	may	have	mild	delays	in	development	of	motor	skills	• Early	testing	is	important	in	helping	a	hearing	impaired	patient	adapt.

Loudness	is	measured	in	decibels	[dB]	• Mild:		Can	hear	sounds	of	20-40	dB	or	louder.		May	have	trouble	hearing	faint	or	distant	speech.		Most	common	cause:		fluid	accumulation	in	middle	ear	(conductive	hearing	loss)	• Moderate:		Can	hear	sounds	of	45-60	dB	or	louder.		Need	speech	to	be	loud.		Difficulty	understanding	speech	in	group	situations.	• Severe:		Can	only	hear	sounds	of	65-85	dB	or	louder. Vasomotor	Rhinits	&	Nasosinusitis	• TCM:		Bi	Yan	[Thick	&	sticky	nasal	discharge	running,	loss	of	the	sense	of	smell,	forehead	ache]	s Lungs	are	mainly	involved	because	lungs	open	to	the	nose.	 Sinuses	&	Differentiation	• Maxillary:		Both	sides	of	cheeks	• Ethmoid:		Deep,	between	eyes	• Sphenoid:		Deep,	behind	nose	à	Headache	deep	in	head,	central	• Frontal:		Above	eyes		Secondary	Points	• Pain	around	eyebrow:		UB	2	à	Yu	Yao	• Headache:		DU	20,	UB	2	• Treatment:		Initial,	Secondary	&	Final	points	for	nasal	congestion	s Step	1:		LI	20,	Bi	Tong	s Step	2:		Nasal	Blockage:		UB	7,	REN	18	[Good	for	nasal	blockage]	s Step	3:		Stubborn	Nasal	Blockage:	DU	23	à	Moxa	until	opens	up	§ Use	stick	moxa	à	3-5	cm	(Don’t	move)	• Chronic	Bi	Yan:		Moxa	Du	23		Other	Treatments	• Auricular:		Inner	Nose,	Forehead,	adrenal,	LU,	endocrine,	Shen	Men	• Herbs	s Loose:			§ Fructus	Xanthii	Cang	Er	Zi	§ Flos	MagnoliaeXin	Yi	Hua	§ Angelica	Dahurica	Bai	Zhu	§ Ligusticum	Sinense	Oliv	Qiang	Huo	§ Radix	Ledebourielae	Fang	Feng	§ Mint	s Patent	herbs	§ Pe	Min	Kan	Wan:		Acute	§ Bi	Yan	Pian/Wan:		Chronic	§ Yin	Qiao:		Wind	Heat	§ Chuan	Xin	Lian	Antiphlogistic	[anti-inflammation]:		Viral	infections	§ She	Dan	Chuan	Bei	Ye:		Post	nasal	discharge	• Point	injection:		Yu	Xing	Cao	[Herba	houttuyniae]	s 0.1	–	0.2	mL	into	points	• Extra	Points	s Shan	Gen	[Foot	of	mountain]:		Between	two	inner	canthus		§ Ethmoid	sinuses	&	stubborn	blockages	à	Combine	with	Yin	Tang	s Shang	Ying	Xiang	[above	LI	20]:		between	softer	part	of	nose	to	bottom	of	nose	[in	groove,	medial	to	LI	20]	à	Used	to	open	nose	s Loss	of	smell:		LI	20,	UB	13,	DU	25	[moxa]	• Ancient	prescriptions	s Rhinitis	§ GB	20,	DU	23,	Yin	Tang,	LI	20,	LI	4	§ GB	20,	UB	10,	DU	23,	LI	20,	LI	4	§ GB	20,	UB	10,	DU	14,	DU	16,	UB	12,	LI	19,	LI	20,	LI	4,	DU	20,	PC	8	s Sinusitis	§ LI	20,	LI	4,	GB	20	§ GB	20,	LI	4,	SI	15,	DU	23,	LI	10,	UB	17,	LI	20	s Loss	of	Smell	§ Point	injection:		UB	13,	LI	20	§ Moxa:		DU	25	
Western	View	[Etiology	&	Pathology]	Rhinitis	 Nasosinusitis	• Inflammation	of	nasomembrane	• Caused	by	small	blood	vessels	in	nose	that	are	sensitive	to	changes	in	environmental	conditions	such	as	temperature,	humidity	&	presence	of	irritants	[smoke,	dryness,	smog,	perfume].		In	response	to	these	conditions	&	irritants,	nose	makes	more	mucus.	
• Inflammation	of	Nasosinuses	• Occurs	when	sinus	membranes	swell	or	become	infected.			• Very	narrow	passageways	between	sinuses	&	from	sinuses	to	nose		• Swelling	of	sinus	linings	causes	excess	mucus	production	• Swelling	&	extra	mucus	may	block	passageways	à	causes	pressure	changes	in	sinuses	à	can	be	painful	• Often	occurs	after	a	cold,	but	not	always.		Other	causes:		Bacteria,	viruses,	allergies,	fungus.	• Injured	or	deformed	nasal	bones	à	partially	blocking	sinus	openings	à	sinusitis	is	more	likely.	Differentiation	• Runny	nose,		• Possible	slight	nasal	congestion	• Feeling	of	fullness	or	pressure	in	head,	headache	painful	when	first	waking	up	in	morning	or	when	bending	head	down	or	forward	• Tenderness	above,	behind	or	below	eyes,	runny	or	stuffy	nose,		• Cough,	especially	at	night		• Sore	throat	in	morning	or	evening,		• Postnasal	drainage,	aching	in	upper	jaw	&	teeth	(may	mistake	for	toothache)	Diagnosis	• No	specific	tests.			• May	swab	lining	of	nose	for		mucus	sample	&	examine	for	eosinophils	(allergy	cells)	[not	always	present	à	test	not	very	helpful]	
• Ask	about	symptoms,	x-ray	to	look	for	swelling,	fluid,	deformed	structure,	polyps	in	sinus	
Treatment	• Nasal	steroid	or	ipratropium	sprays	for	treatment	&	prevention	• Antibiotics	&	decongestants	for	several	weeks,		• Acetaminophen	or	aspirin	for	pain	• Antihistamines	or	nasal	sprays	(steroids	or	cromolyn)	for	allergies	• Chronic,	Severe	Sinusitis:		Surgery	to	create	extra	or	enlarged	passageways	in	sinus	cavity	walls.		Allows	sinuses	to	drain	more	easily	through	nasal	passages.	• Significant	Symptoms	for	rhinitis	&	sinusitis:		Nasal	obstruction,	loss	of	sense	of	smell,	profuse	nasal	discharge,	dull	pain	in	forehead.	• Differentiate	hypotrophic	rhinitis	&	vasomotor	rhinitis:	s Apply	0.1%	adrenaline	in	nose	§ Vasomotor:		Gets	better	immediately	with	adrenaline	§ Hypotrophic:		Doesn’t	get	better	with	adrenaline			 Wind	Cold	 Wind	Heat/Lung	Heat	 Fire	in	LV/GB	Symptoms	• Chills	>>	Fever,	itchy	nose	• Sneezing,	runny	nos	 • Fever	>>	Chills	• Thick,	yellow,	sticky	nasal	discharge	• Cough	 • Fetid/yellow	nasal	discharge	• Irritability,	Bitter	taste	in	mouth	• Dry	throat,	Headache	Tongue	• 	 • Red,	red	tip	 • Red,	dry,	yellow	coating	Pulse	• Tight,	floating	 • Rapid,	floating	 • Rapid,	floating	pulse	Treatment	Principle	• Disperse	Wind	Cold	• Clear	Lungs	&	Nasal	cavities	• LU/LI	points:	à	smooth	flow	of	LU	Qi,	expel	pathogenic	wind	cold	by	reducing.	
• Disperse	Wind	Heat	• Clear	Lungs	&	Nasal	cavities	• LU/LI	points:	à	smooth	flow	of	LU	Qi,	expel	pathogenic	wind	heat	by	reducing.	
• Induce	LV/GB	fire	downward	
Prescription	• Main:		LU	7,	LI	20,	LI	4,	Bi	Tong,	Yin	Tang	• GB	20,	UB	10,	11,	12	 • Main:		LU	7,	LI	20,	LI	4,	Bi	Tong,	Yin	Tang	• LI	11,	LU	5,	UB	13,	DU	23	 • Main:		LU	7,	LI	20,	LI	4,	Bi	Tong,	Yin	Tang	• LV	2,	GB	34

	Epistaxis	• Epistaxis	(nosebleed)	occurs	when	the	membranes	lining	the	inner	nose	are	disturbed	or	irritated	enough	to	cause	abnormal	bleeding.	• TCM:	Bi	Nu	[Nu	=	bleeding]	s LU,	ST,	KD	mainly	involved		Common	causes:		Breakdown	in	membranes	lining	the	nose	• Can	be	triggered	by	dry	air,	probing,	bumping,	picking	or	rubbing	nose	• Blowing	nose	forcefully	can	also	cause	nosebleed,	especially	if	nasal	membranes	are	already	inflamed	due	to	allergies	or	infection.	• Injury	to	face	or	nose,	high	altitude,	high	blood	pressure	• Re-bleeding	from	an	area	of	previous	nosebleed	• Medicine	that	increases	clotting	time	• Nosebleed	after	heavy	blow	to	skull	could	mean	fractured	skull	à	ER		First	Aid	• When	nose	starts	bleeding,	sit	up	&	lean	forward	to	prevent	blood	from	passing	into	throat	• Pinch	nose	firmly	together	between	thumb	&	index	finger,	just	below	nasal	bones,	hold	for	10	minutes.	• After	bleeding	stops,	use	saline	nasal	spray	or	nose	drops	to	keep	nose	moist.			• Do	not	blow	nose	for	several	hours.	• If	a	nosebleed	lasts	>	20	minutes	or	does	not	respond	to	first	aid	à	go	to	MD	s Will	likely	apply	cotton	ball	soaked	in	epinephrine,	or	nose	drops	like	Neo-Synephrine	or	Afrin	to	bleeding	site	for	5-10	minutes	to	numb	&	temporarily	reduce	blood	supply	to	nasal	membrane.	s Then	pack	nose	with	Vaseline	coated	gauze	or	special	balloon	to	provide	pressure	s Can	also	use	cauterization	to	force	blood	to	coagulate	at	bleeding	site.		Will	help	prevent	future	bleeding.

	Toothache	• Common	ailment	à	Pain	in	or	around	tooth	• Pain	causes:		Infected	tooth,	infected	gum,	irritated	nerve,	problem	with	sinuses	• TCM:		Huo	Ya	s ST	&	Wind	Fire,	KD	deficiency		Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Tooth	covered	with	calcified	enamel	that	protects	soft	living	center	of	the	tooth,	which	contains	the	blood	supply,	nerve	&	pulp	(tissue	surrounding	blood	vessels	&	nerve)	• Pain	may	occur	if	patient	has	tooth	decay,	which	may	irritate	the	nerve	or	may	lead	to	bacterial	infection	of	pulp,	which	can	be	very	painful.	• Tooth’s	nerve	can	be	irritated	by	fracture	or	crack.	• Patient	may	get	toothache	if	a	filling	in	tooth	is	damaged	• Repetitive	motions	such	as	chewing	gum	more	than	usual	or	grinding	teeth	during	sleep	can	also	cause	damage	&	pain	s ST	heat,	fire,	parasites	• Meridians	[NCCAOM]	s ST	goes	to	upper	gum	s LI	goes	to	lower	gum		Differentiation	• Tooth	pain	may	be	sharp,	throbbing	or	constant	or	may	hurt	only	when	patient	puts	pressure	on	tooth.			• Patient	may	have	swelling	around	tooth	or	get	fever	or	headache.	• Sometimes	foul	tasting	drainage	from	an	infected	tooth	• If	patient	with	toothache	has	teeth	in	good	condition	à	examine	ear,	throat,	jaw,	or	sinuses.	• KD	Deficiency:		Teeth	get	loose	[gums	receding]

Urinary	Incontinence	Definition:	Accidental	urine	leakage	while	in	conscious	condition	• May	be	more	common	as	a	person	ages	but	is	not	a	normal	part	of	aging	• Nocturnal	Enuresis:	Accidental	urine	leakage	while	in	unconscious	condition	during	sleeping.		Usually	related	to	young	children.	• More	often	occurs	in	females	than	males		s Frequent	with	new	moms	à	pressure	during	pregnancy	(KD	Qi/Yang	Xu)	s Male:		prostate	surgery	or	low	back	herniated	disc	surgery	• Fracture	of	hip	or	surgery	in	hip	area	• 20%	senior	citizens	• Main	TCM	organs	involved	in	water	metabolism:		LU,	SP,	KD		Western	Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Stress	Incontinence:		Lifting	objects,	sneezing,	coughing,	laughing	[long	history	of	disease	like	chronic	cough]	• Urgent	Incontinence:		Urge	to	urinate	so	strong	that	can’t	make	it	to	toilet	in	time	(overactive	bladder	à	nerve,	muscle)	[Bladder	infection]	• Overflow	Incontinence:		Bladder	doesn’t	empty	as	it	should	&	then	leaks	urine	later.		Happens	when	bladder	muscles	are	weak.		Usually	have	urge	at	300	cc.		Bladder	can	usually	hold	up	to	800	cc.	• Total	Incontinence:		Bladder	is	always	leaking	urine.		Sphincter	muscle	no	longer	works.	• Involuntary	Incontinence:		Unaware	of	incontinence	• Functional	Incontinence:		Can’t	make	it	to	bathroom	in	time	to	urinate.		Not	common.		Hold	it	&	can’t	go	to	bathroom	on	time.	• Enuresis/Nocturnal	Enuresis:		Occurs	at	night	while	person	sleeping	(bedwetting)	
Clinical	Manifestation	[Western	Medicine]	Most	common	sign	of	urinary	incontinence	is	leaking	urine	from	bladder. 	Male	Infertility	Definition:		Inability	of	a	male	to	achieve	pregnancy	in	a	fertile	female	after	one	year	of	unprotected	intercourse.		In	humans,	it	accounts	for	40-50%	of	infertility.	TCM	Main	Organ:		KD		Western	Medicine	factors	related	to	male	infertility:	• Pre-testicular	• Testicular	• Post-testicular		Pre-Testicular	Factors:		Conditions	that	impede	adequate	support	of	the	testes	• Obesity:		Can	lead	to	hormone	issues	[Hypogonadism	(diminished	functional	activity	of	gonads)	à	Lower	male	hormones	à	é	risk	of	male	breast	cancer	• Drugs	• Alcohol:		Interferes	with	DNA	methylation	• Tobacco	smoking:		30%	higher	chance	of	infertility	[Cadmium	toxins	(absorbed	from	soil)	in	tobacco	replace	zinc.		Male	sexual	organs	rely	on	zinc	à	decreased	zinc	levels	à	semen	quality	ê]	• Strenuous	riding:			s Bicycling	long	distance	à	small	seat	à	pressure	on	blood	supply	to	testes		s Tight	shorts	or	underwear	à	testes	too	close	to	body	à	too	warm	• Medication:		Anti-depresants,	chemotherapy,	steroids	• Genetic	abnormalities	[Robertsonian	translocation]:		participating	chromosomes	break	at	their	centromeres	and	the	long	arms	fuse	to	form	a	single,	large	chromosome	with	a	single	centromere.	They	generally	occur	in	the	five	acrocentric	chromosome	pairs,	namely	13,	14,	15,	21	and	22. Impotence	[Erectile	Dysfunction	(ED)]	• Male	sexual	dysfunction	characterized	by	persistent	inability	to	develop	or	maintain	an	erection	of	the	penis	satisfactory	during	sexual	performance.		s 15-30	million	ED	patients	in	US.	s Needs	to	be	longer	than	7	minutes.		ED	patients:		average	2	minutes	• TCM	Name:		Yang	Wei,	Yin	Wei,	Jin	Wei	(Jin	=	tendon)	• TCM	concept:		ED	occurs	only	occurs	in	males	<	8x8	(64	years	old)	[Tian	Gui]		Western	Etiology	• Penile	erection	based	on	physiological	phenomenon	where	the	human	penis	becomes	firm	&	enlarged	s Causes:		complex	interaction	of	psychological,	neural,	vascular	&	endocrine	factors,	usually	associated	with	sexual	arousal	or	sexual	attraction.			s Erectile	dysfunction	is	indicated	when	an	erection	is	difficult	to	achieve	• Clinically,	2	types:	s Psychogenic	causes:		10-20%	à	better	prognosis	s Disease	cause:		70%			§ Common	causative	diseases:		Hypertension,	atherosclerosis,	cholesterol	deposits	in	blood	vessels	(many	block	small	arteries	first	à	penis	has	smaller	arteries	than	heart,	DM,	etc.	§ Dietary:		low	potassium	(eat	bananas,	avocados),	arsenic	(brown	rice	à	3x	higher	than	white	rice)		Western	Treatment	• Physician	evaluates	the	condition	in	order	to	determine	the	specific	cause	of	erectile	dysfunction. Urinary	Retention	• Definition:		Situation	in	which	Urinary	Bladder	is	either	completely	unable	to	void	or	can	only	partially	void.	s Long:		Urinary	Bladder	is	full	of	urine	but	can	only	partially	void.	s Bi:		Urinary	Bladder	is	full	of	urine	but	is	unable	to	void.		• Bladder	Maximum	Capacity:		800	cc	s 300-500	cc:		Strong	urge	to	urinate	s 150	cc:		Can	feel	the	pressure		Western	Etiology	&	Pathogenesis	• Prostate	Enlargement:		50-60s	(Urethra	goes	through	prostate)	• Cystocele:		Wall	between	woman’s	bladder	&	vagina	weakens	&	allows	bladder	to	protrude	through.	• Rectocele:		Rectum	drops	into	the	back	wall	of	the	vagina	• Nerve	Damage:		If	nerves	aren’t	working,	the	brain	may	not	get	the	message	that	the	bladder	is	full	(brain/spine	injury,	infection	in	pelvic	area,	MS	à	prognosis	not	good)	• Urinary	Tract	Infection	(UTI)	• Anesthesia	in	surgery	• Medication:		Stop	taking	medication	• Bladder	stones:		Remove	stones	• Urethral	stricture	• Scarring	of	bladder	neck:		Injury/surgery		Long	Term	Effects	of	Urinary	Tract	Obstruction	• Bladder	stones	• Loss	of	detrusor	muscle	tone,	Hypertrophy	of	detrusor	muscle	• Hydronephrosis:		Swelling	of	a	kidney	due	to	a	build-up	of	urine.
