Tuberculosis	• Definition:		Contagious	bacterial	infection	that	involves	lungs,	but	may	spread	to	other	organs	[Can	be	anywhere	in	body]	§ Agricultural	countries:		Most	common	is	in	knee	§ Developing	countries:		Can	affect	spine	• Known	since	ancient	Egypt.		Most	common	mode	of	transmission	in	US	is	inhalation	of	infected	airborne	droplets.		Initial	TB	infection	can	manifest	clinically	as	the	disease,	but	usually	presents	with	no	symptoms	because	the	immune	system	controls	the	bacteria.			• Recrudescence	(reactivation)	of	dormant	bacteria	is	most	common	clinical	presentation	of	TB	• TCM	Organs:		LU	(primary),	can	also	involve	SP	&	KD	• Transmission:		Saliva,	cough,	food	s Most	TB	bacteria	killed	in	stomach,	unless	ingested	in	large	amounts	s Bacteria	multiply	slowly	à	10-20	hours	to	make	next	generation	à	Antibodies	can	fight	&	win	s Bacteria	killed	easily	(no	flying	sword	sputum):		Cough	sputum	into	paper	&	burn	it	à	Bacteria	die	in	strong	sunlight	in	2-7	hours.	§ Put	bedding	in	yard	in	sunlight	for	a	whole	day	§ Must	boil	tableware	for	4-5	hours	§ 70%	alcohol:		5-30	minutes	to	kill	bacteria	[Rubbing	not	enough	to	kill]	§ Most	Asian	kids	get	immunized	for	TB	§ Autoclave		Risk	Factors	for	Active	TB	• Living	in	crowded	or	unsanitary	living	conditions	• Chronic	cough	or	on	an	airplane	• Weakened	immune	systems	(AIDS,	chemotherapy,	diabetes,	people	taking	certain	medications,	steroids,	etc.)	• Elderly	• Infants	• Frequent	contact	with	TB	patients:		8-10	hours	• Poor	nutrition	• Medical	providers		TB	Categories	• Active	pulmonary	tuberculosis:		Sputum	contains	M.	tuberculosis	à	contagious	• Dormant	(Latent)	Pulmonary	tuberculosis:		Sputum	does	not	contain	M.	tuberculosis	à	Not	infectious		TB	Transmission	Modes	• Direct	Infection	s Direct	inhalation	of	active	tuberculosis	in	air	droplets	spraying	from	nose	&	mouth	from	patients	coughing,	sneezing,	loud	conversation	[Most	important	mode	of	transmission]	s Kissing	active	pulmonary	tuberculosis	patients	s Using	tableware	&	consuming	food	polluted	by	the	patient	[lower	chance	of	infection]	s Bacteria	can	live	on	surfaces	for	8-10	days	• Indirect	Infection	s Indirect	inhalation	of	air	droplets	containing	TB.		Often	occurs	in	poorly	ventilated	rooms.	s Can	survive	in	dirt	for	6	months.	s Inhalation	of	dust	contaminated	with	tuberculosis.		Contaminated	by	sputum	of	patients	on	floors,	clothing,	bedding.			s Not	every	contact	with	tuberculosis	will	contract	TB.		Only	people	with	poor	resistance	are	more	susceptible	to	TB	infection.		Pulmonary	Symptoms	• Cough:		Chicken	cough	[Short,	loud,	sounds	like	a	shrill	sneeze],	stimulating	cough	• Chest	pain:		Dull	to	stabbing,	depends	on	stage	• Sputum:		Dry,	sticky,	may	have	fresh	red	blood		• Hemoptysis

Benign	Prostatic	Hyperplasia	• Prostate	Location:		Walnut	sized	gland	located	between	the	bladder	and	penis	(only	in	dogs	and	adult	human	males),	just	in	front	of	the	rectum.		The	urethra	runs	through	the	center	of	the	prostate,	from	the	bladder	to	the	penis,	letting	urine	flow	out	of	the	body.	• Prostate	Function:		Secretes	fluid	that	nourishes	&	protects	sperm.		During	ejaculation,	the	prostate	squeezes	this	fluid	into	the	urethra	and	it’s	expelled	with	sperm	as	semen.	• Benign	Prostatic	Hyperplasia	or	Hypertrophy	(BPH):		Enlarged	prostate	gland.		Occurs	in	almost	all	men	as	they	age,	but	not	necessarily	a	problem	of	aging.		Western	Medicine	• Etiology	&	Pathogenesis:		Presence	of	DHT	(dihydrotestosterone:		male	hormone)	stimulates	growth	factors	in	the	prostate,	leading	to	hyperplasia	with	a	resulting	increase	in	size.		Prostate	Tests	• Digital	Rectal	Examination	(DRE)	• Prostate	Specific	Antigen	(PSA)	s PSA	>	10	ng/mL	à	biopsy	s Between	2.5	–	10	ng/mL	§ Normal	DRE	à	fPSA	test	§ Abnormal	DRE	à	biopsy	• fPSA	(Free,	unbound	PSA):		High	%	fPSA	=	good	news	s fPSA	≤	7%	à	Biopsy	immediately	s fPSA:		7%	-	15%	à	Debatable	§ Most	men	with	prostate	cancer	have	fPSA	<	15%	§ Some	doctors	will	recommend	biopsy	immediately	§ Others	doctors	recommend	6-12	months	for	another	test	s fPSA:		15%	-	25%	à	Wait	one	year	for	another	PSA	and	fPSA	test	§ Allows	PSA	velocity	to	be	determined	[>	0.5	ng/mL	per	year	à	cancer]	§ Allows	refinement	of	fPSA	reading	s fPSA	>	25%	à	Likely	benign	§ Repeat	PSA	and	DRE	at	annual	exam	• Prostate	Ultrasound	• Prostate	Biopsy

Multiple	Sclerosis	• Definition:		An	autoimmune	disease	that	affects	the	central	nervous	system	(brain	&	spinal	cord).		First	described	in	1868	by	Jean-Martin	Charcot.			s Affects	women	more	than	men	s Commonly	diagnosed	between	age	20	–	40,	but	can	be	seen	at	any	age.	s Affects	400,000	in	the	US,	1	million	patients	worldwide	• TCM	Organs:		LU,	SP,	KD,	LV	Western	Etiology	&	Pathogenesis:		Unknown	cause	• Most	common	theory:		Virus,	gene	defect	or	both	to	blame	• Environmental	factors	may	play	a	role	• Inflammation	causes	the	myelin	sheath	to	disappear,	which	slows	the	electrical	impulses	that	travel	along	the	nerves.		It	also	damages	the	nerves.	• Myelin:		Mainly	located	in	the	white	matter	in	the	brain.		When	lost,	the	myelin	creates	scars	in	the	white	matter	and	the	axons	no	longer	effectively	conduct	signals.	s Brain:		MS	affects	white	matter	s Demyelination:		Slows	down	nerve	transmission	in	axon	• There	is	a	25-35%	chance	of	heredity	in	getting	MS.	• In	1996,	the	United	States	National	Multiple	Sclerosis	Society	standardized	four	clinical	courses	(stages).	
Western	Medicine	Clinical	Manifestation	• Patients	with	MS	can	have	symptoms	in	many	parts	of	the	body.	s Damage	to	nerve	fibers	of	brain	stem	&	cerebellum	can	cause	double	vision,	difficulty	swallowing,	gait	disturbance	or	dizziness.	s Inflammation	of	spinal	cord:		Affects	motor,	sensory	&	excretion	functions	s Nerve	damage	closer	to	the	spinal	cord	will	affect	the	limbs	s Small	number	of	patients	may	also	suffer	epilepsy	or	mental	retardation	Symptoms	Muscle	Symptoms	• Loss	of	balance,	Muscle	spasms	• Numbness/abnormal	sensation	(located	in	any	area)	• Problems	moving	arms	or	legs	• Problems	walking,	with	coordination	or	making	small	movements	• Tremors	or	weakness	in	one	or	more	arms/legs	Bowel	&	Bladder	symptoms	• Constipation,	stool	leakage	• Urine	leakage	(incontinence)	• Urination:		Difficulty	initiating,	Frequent	need,	Strong	urge	Eye	Symptoms	• Double	vision,	Eye	Discomfort	• Uncontrollable	rapid	eye	movements	• Vision	Loss	(usually	affects	one	eye	at	a	time)	
Numbness,	tingling,	pain	• Facial	pain,	Painful	muscle	spasms		• Tingling,	crawling	or	burning	feeling	in	arms	&	legs	Other	brain	&	nerve	symptoms	• Decreased	attention	span	• Poor	judgment,	memory	loss	• Difficulty	reasoning,	problem	solving	• Depression,	feelings	of	sadness	• Dizziness	&	balance	problems	• Hearing	loss	Sexual	Symptoms	• Problems	with	erections	• Problems	with	vaginal	lubrication	Speech	&	swallowing	symptoms	• Slurred,	difficult	to	understand	speech	• Trouble	chewing	&	swallowing	Fatigue:		Common	&	bothersome	symptom.		Worse	in	late	afternoon.	
Four	Clinical	Stages	Relapsing-Remitting	(RRMS)	• Characterized	by	unpredictable	relapses	followed	by	periods	of	months	to	years	of	remission	with	no	new	signs	of	disease	activity.		Deficits	suffered	during	attacks	may	either	resolve	or	leave	sequelae	[conditions	that	are	the	consequences	of	a	previous	disease	or	injury].	• 50-60%	of	patients,	often	diagnosed	between	20	–	40	years	(younger	age)	• Can	be	totally	gone	(no	symptoms)	and	then	come	back	Secondary	Progressive	• Can	take	15-20	years	to	develop	into	this	from	RR	type	• Once	patients	have	symptoms,	they	progressively	get	worse.		• More	than	half	the	patients	that	start	with	RR	will	turn	into	progressive.		The	attacks	get	worse	and	closer	until	they	eventually	become	just	one	attack	with	no	remission.	• Patients	may	still	experience	relapses	(attacks).	These	are	then	followed	by	partial	recoveries	or	periods	of	remission,	but	the	disease	doesn’t	disappear	between	cycles.	Steadily	worsens.	
Factors	affecting	prognosis	• Older	than	35	for	first	attack	• Various	parts	of	nervous	system	damaged	in	first	episode	• GI	excretory	function	disorders	are	main	symptom	of	recurrence	• Recurrence	more	than	twice	in	first	year	• Relapse	after	remission	not	complete		Chinese	Medicine	Category:		Wei	Syndrome	• External	Pathogenic	Factor	Invasions	s Damp	Invasion:		Damp	is	most	common	reason	for	autoimmune	disorders	s Wind	Heat:		Strong	type	of	wind	heat	with	high	fever	type	§ Heat	drains	yin	fluid	§ Most	MS	patients	suffer	from	yin	deficiency	§ Diet	with	damp	heat	will	drain	yin	à	Adhere	to	strict	diet	• Diet:		Avoid	Damp	food	&	excessive	consumption	of	Fa	Wu	(sea	food,	onions,	garlic,	deep-fried,	duck	(cool	but	fa	wu)	à	Fa	Wu	causes	more	inflammation	s Burn	ST	36,	eat	Fa	Wu	à	Produce	pus	à	Helps	immune	system	• Excessive	Sexual	Activity:		Drains	KD	Yin	fluid,	Depletes	KD	• Shock	&	Severe	Emotions:		HT	&	SP	take	the	biggest	hit.		Treatment	Principles	[RRMS	most	frequently	seen	in	clinic.]	• Most	MS	patients	are	deficient	à	Don’t	use	big	needles	or	too	many	needles	s Most	Needle	Sensitive:		Patients	in	wheelchair	from	progressive	type,	Patients	with	bowel	dysfunction	that	won’t	improve,	etc.	s Poor	results	with	too	many	needles/points	• Active	Attacks	or	Newly	Diagnosed	Patient:	s Main	Focus:		Releasing	body	surface,	expelling	wind,	clearing	heat,	resolving	phlegm,	activating	blood,	unblocking	meridians	s Treat	locally	and	economically	during	active	attacks.		s Try	to	reverse	progression	of	disease.	s Treat	symptoms:		Limb	issues	à	treat	limbs,	Damp	à	Treat	damp,	etc.	s Treat	locally	because	it	triggers	the	brain	and	prolongs	the	life	of	the	myelin	sheath.	• Remission	Period	or	Late	Stages:	s Main	Focus:		Fortifying	KD,	Replenishing	essence,	Soothing	LV,	Nourishing	blood,	Supplementing	Qi	(vital	energy),	Reinforcing	SP.			s Base	treatment	on	syndrome	differentiation.	s Too	much	stimulation	can	cause	relapse.	s Treat	with	herbs	to	build	up	SP	Qi,	Resolve	damp,	Nourish	LV	&	KD	yin.	
Primary	Progressive	• 10-25%	of	patients	• Neurological	function	steadily	degrades.		Once	they	have	symptoms,	they	just	become	worse.		No	remission	(No	stairs).	Progressive	Relapsing	• Very	rare,	not	common.		5-10%	of	patients.	• Sustained	neurological	degradation	with	intense	attacks.		No	steady	period	of	stability.		Sometimes	gets	a	little	better.			• Patients	often	end	up	in	a	wheelchair	very	quickly.	Western	Medicine	Current	theories	• Possibly	due	to	viral	infection	(possibly	herpes)	in	genetically	susceptible	people,	combined	with	environment,	timing	and	age.	• Geographical	s High	Latitude:		Incidence	rates	higher	above	40°	latitude,	worse	in	west	s Very	low	incidence	near	equator	in	tropical	regions	• Dietary:			Saturated	fat	&	dairy	(major	component)	à	Damp.			s Higher	incidence	in	populations	with	high	saturated	fat	intake.			s 5%	died	with	<	20g	saturated	fat	daily.	s 80%	died	with	>	20g	saturated	fat	daily	s Need	B-12,	but	get	from	sources	other	than	red	meat.	• Genetic:		Higher	incidence	in	first-degree	relatives	of	patients	with	MS	(3-5%)	s Incidence	in	dizygotic	(fraternal)	twins:		3-5%	s Incidence	in	monozygotic	(identical)	twins:		25%	s People	of	Scandinavian	descent	have	50%	increase	of	getting	MS.	• Misdiagnosis:	Common	in	beginning	of	MS	due	to	symptom	localization	s Visual	impairment:		More	than	40%	of	patients	have	initial	symptoms	of	visual	impairment	s Limb	paralysis:		Paraplegia	accompanied	with	urination	difficulty	often	misdiagnosed	as	myelitis	Western	Medicine	Clinical	Manifestation	• Symptoms	vary	because	the	location	and	severity	of	each	attack	can	be	different.		Episodes	can	last	for	days,	weeks	or	months.	These	episodes	alternate	with	periods	of	reduced	or	no	symptoms	(remissions).	• Fever,	hot	baths,	sun	exposure	and	stress	can	trigger	or	worsen	attacks.	• Relapses	(return	of	the	disease)	are	common.		However,	the	disease	may	continue	to	get	worse	without	periods	of	remission.

Fibromyalgia	History	• 1976:	researcher	Hench	noted	syndrome	&	introduced	term	fibromyalgia	• 1990:	American	College	of	Rheumatology	established	firm	criteria	for	classification	&	diagnosis	of	fibromyalgia	• US:		Over	5	million	fibromyalgia	patients	(2%	of	population)		Western	Etiology	&	Pathology:		Causative	nature	unclear	• Possible	malfunction	of	central	nervous	system	(serotonin	&	glutamate)	s MSG:		Originally	from	yam	s Beef/pork	broth:		Have	natural	MSG	• Some	in	medical	profession	think	it	has	psychological	nature	(laziness)	• Possible	muscle	problem,	but	research	studies	found	nothing	wrong	in	muscles	• Possible	autoimmune	problem,	but	research	revealed	no	immune	system	disturbance	• Observations	in	fibromyalgia	patients	s Lower	cortisol	levels	s Swollen	capillaries	in	muscles	(under	microscope)	s Burgdorferi	virus	infection	
TCM	Treatment	Principles	• Promote	local	blood	circulation	(tendons	don’t	have	good	circulation)	• Disperse	fluids	• Remove	blockage	• Tonify	Qi	&	Blood	• Stop	Pain		Points	used	for	each	local	tender	spot	according	to	ACR	18	spots	• Neck	Pain:		SI	3,	UB	10,	DU	16,	Bai	Lao	• Shoulder	Pain:		LI	15,	Jian	Qian,		s Jian	Hou	(1.5	cun	above	posterior	axillary	crease)	• Arm	Pain:		LI	11,	SJ	5,	LI	4	• Upper	Back	Pain:		UB	11,	UB	17,	SI	12	• Lower	Back	Pain:		UB	23,	UB	25,	UB	40	• Thigh	Pain:		SP	10,	GB	31,	LV	8	• Leg	Pain:		ST	36,	GB	39,	SP	6		Auricular	Points	• Shen	Men:		Sleep	• LV:		Move	Qi	• HT:		Circulation	• SP	• Sympathetic:		Calm	nerves,	promote	ciruculation		Tui	Na	[é	circulation,	Help	supply	O2]	• Very	effective	in	addition	to	acupuncture	&	herbs.	• Move	joints	à	Loosen	up	• Research	has	shown	significant	decrease	in	pain	level	with	Tui	Na	• Some	practitioners	consider	Tui	Na	as	main	TCM	approach	for	fibromyalgia	treatment	• Common	techniques	s Grasping:		release	muscle	tightness	(é	circulation,	not	release	adhesion)	s Kneading:		Tonifies	Qi	&	blood	s Rolling:		Reduces	inflammation	in	vessels	inside	muscles	s Chafing:		Promotes	blood	circulation	(best	to	make	area	warm/red)		Other	Techniques	• Seven	star	needle:		Around	tender	points	[é	circulation,	tap	to	red,	no	bleed]	• Cupping:		Around	tender	points	[They	love	the	cupping]	• Gua	Sha:		Around	tender	points	[é	circulation,	especially	tendons]		
Manifestations	Symptoms	• Widespread	pain	(Main	determining	factor	for	diagnosis)	• Fatigue,	chronic	headache	• Insomnia	(90%),	Sleep	apnea	• Inability	to	fall	into	deep	sleep	• Abdominal	pain,	gastric	distress	• Dry	mouth,	Inability	to	concentrate	• Restless	Leg	Syndrome

• SP	3,	SP	10,	GB	20,	DU	20	• ST	36,	ST	40	• UB	23,	UB	17	• Auricular:		SP,	HT,	Adrenal,	Endocrine		Lifestyle	• Establish	regular	sleep	routines	s Sleep	before	11	pm,	wake	up	at	6	am	à	Go	outside	&	get	better	energy	s Just	get	outside	à	Can	go	outside	&	sleep	on	chair	• Tips	for	good	sleep	habits	s Avoid	exercising	for	at	least	6	hours	before	bedtime	s Use	bed	only	for	sleep,	not	an	office	s Avoid	caffeine	&	alcohol	4-6	hours	before	bedtime	s Avoid	large	meals	before	bedtime.		Light	snack	might	help	sleep.	s Avoid	naps,	especially	in	evening	or	late	afternoon.	s Establish	regular	time	for	going	to	bed	&	getting	up	in	morning.	s Maintain	regular	schedule	even	on	weekends	or	during	vacations	s If	unable	to	fall	asleep	after	15-20	minutes,	go	into	another	room	and	start	a	quiet	activity.		Return	to	bed	when	you	feel	sleepy.	s Avoid	drinking	fluids	right	before	bedtime	so	that	you	don’t	have	to	wake	to	urinate.	s Minimize	light	&	maintain	a	comfortable,	moderate	temperature	in	the	bedroom.		Keep	bedroom	well	ventilated.	• Reduce	stress	s Develop	a	plan	to	avoid	or	limit	overexertion	&	emotional	stress	s Allow	time	each	day	to	relax,	and	learn	to	say	no	without	guilt.	s Try	not	to	completely	change	routine.		People	who	quit	work	or	drop	all	activity	do	worse	than	those	who	stay	active.	s Try	stress	management	techniques	like	deep-breathing	exercises	or	meditation.	• Exercise	regularly.		At	first,	exercise	may	increase	pain.		Doing	it	gradually	&	regularly	often	decreases	symptoms.	
Other	Recommendations	• Appropriate	Exercise	• Tai	Chi	is	most	effective	exercise	for	fibromyalgia	s Promotes	circulation	of	Qi	&	Blood		s Stretches	muscles	&	tendons	to	stop	pain	• Yoga,	Qi	Gong	&	water	exercise	also	good	for	fibromyalgia	• Write	down	length	&	time	(poor	memory)	Dietary	Foods	to	Avoid	 Recommended	Foods	• Caffeine	(make	more	tired)	• Yeast	(damp)	• Gluten	• MSG	• Trans	Fat	• Alcoholic	beverages	• Carbonated	food	&	beverages	• Chocolate	(sugar)	• High	Fat	Dairy	foods	• Fried	foods	(Fa	Wu)	• Preservatives,	junk	food	• Food	colors	&	dyes	• Tobacco	• Crabs	(Fa	Wu)	à	Acne	• Sugar	• Pork	(interferes	with	medication,	even	for	common	cold)
